When should a STEM undergrad start to consider a post-grad?
2026-09-21

When should a STEM undergrad start to consider a post-grad?

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
A master’s or post-grad isn’t always necessary for a long career, but how do you know when it is the logical next step? Master’s and post-grad programmes are designed to give students an advanced and often specialised education, so when the time comes to begin a career, they have the necessary skillset and qualifications. But not every STEM career requires an additional degree. More and more organisations are hiring based on talent and suitability, not just on where you went to college or the courses you undertook. Some people may want to jump straight into their professional life after college and others may feel that they need a little more support, guidance and knowledge before taking that step. So, with that in mind, when might another educational programme be the right move for an individual looking to pursue a career in a STEM field? In almost every industry, once an undergraduate degree is completed, most people have the skills and qualifications they need to start applying for jobs in their chosen field and often do so. Many large STEM companies specifically have internship programmes designed to support the career ambitions of recent graduates, so they can learn the ins and outs of the business. But sometimes, people may finish an undergraduate degree in the STEM space and not have the specifics required to move into the role they actually want. They might find that their previous education, while informative, was perhaps too broad for them to appeal to recruiters or employers. Post-grad programmes are an ideal way to significantly deepen your knowledge of often overlooked or niche fields, helping you stand out from the crowd when it comes to job-hunting – or even applying for a PhD. A more specialised education in AI and how it impacts other STEM fields, for example, can often be a game-changer for professionals, as evidenced by a recent report that indicated more than half of AI-related role postings are looking for skills beyond what the job title suggests, resulting in new hybrid roles. Life-changing career opportunities for you Build a future you believe in. Join our team A master’s or post-grad programme can give you access to a wider range of skills and future career routes, but it can also set you up for a role that offers greater remuneration in terms of titles and financial compensation. While an organisation might accept both undergraduate students and those who have graduated from a master’s programme around the same time for similar roles, jobseekers with additional qualifications can make a stronger argument for why their benefits package and pay should be greater than that of someone with less experience or education. Whether it is warranted or not, an employer may also consider candidates with a master’s for promotions and added responsibilities ahead of someone without one, simply as a policy and not necessarily in relation to the work already being carried out. It may also be a financially sound opportunity in the moment if you currently don’t have the means to continue your education, as many post-graduate programmes come with funding, particularly those in the STEM space. Funded post-graduate education or research positions are not always easy to secure and they often require a significant commitment. Therefore, having earned and completed one can also show an employer that you are competitive, hard-working and focused. It may well be that after you have completed your undergraduate education, you feel as though you have far more to contribute. A post-graduate degree gives you the chance to carry out your own research and test your own theories, with the hope of having a paper or results to publish in the end. It is an ideal opportunity to make your mark in the STEM space and can open up many doors post-college; for example, it can lead to a career in academia, or can be a great stepping stone towards a STEM-focused company that depends on forward-thinking and innovative people. At the end of the day, choosing to undertake an additional degree after you have completed your undergraduate course is all a matter of circumstance. It is highly dependent on your current skillset, the career and title you are aiming for, the lifestyle you would prefer – but also what you can feasibly afford and commit to now. While it might sound like a lot to consider, once you have, your decision should be relatively easy. And remember, you can always go back to college, regardless of where you are at in your professional life – the door is always open. Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Galway scientists bet on 3D-printed coral reefs to revitalise marine life
2026-09-21

Galway scientists bet on 3D-printed coral reefs to revitalise marine life

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
University of Galway researchers attaching coral to the artificial reef. Image: Redecor ‘We cannot simply wait for them to recover on their own,’ says Ifremer expert Marie-Claire Fabri. Scientists are hoping 3D-printed coral reefs can help revive deep-sea habitats destroyed by bottom trawling. Researchers from the University of Galway, Sorbonne University and the French oceanographic institution Ifremer, are testing 23 artificial reefs placed on the seabed off the south-west Porcupine Bank – 200km off the west coast of Ireland – and in the Bay of Biscay, France. The tests are a part of a three-year joint collaboration between the three institutions called Restauration Des Coraux d’eau froide (Redecor). As part of the mission, scientists were deployed on an expedition aboard a vessel from the French Oceanographic Fleet this August, where they placed 3D-printed reefs at depths of between 900m and 1,100m off the Porcupine Bank and in the Guilvinec Canyon, Bay of Biscay. Despite being considered lesser known than their tropical counterparts, cold-water coral reefs provide important habitats for marine life and support biodiversity in the deep sea. However, these coral reefs are easily damaged by deep sea trawling. And despite a ban on trawling in many coral-rich areas, the fragile habitat requires additional efforts to revive to full health. “These deep-sea coral reefs provide vital habitat for a diversity of marine life, but many have been damaged over many years,” said Dr Anthony Grehan, a deep-sea ecologist at University of Galway and the co-leader of the mission to Porcupine Bank. “Because these corals grow by only a few millimetres each year, natural recovery of lost reefs can take decades to centuries. “With this project, we are testing whether we can actively accelerate that recovery by creating new starter structures for corals to grow on which also mimic reef structure to help re-establish functioning reef habitats.” Testing artificial reefs as a viable method to revitalise sea life is one of the ways scientists in the bloc are attempting to tackle marine degradation as part of a four-year Horizon Europe project called ‘Restoration of deep-sea habitats to rebuild European Seas’, or Redress. The artificial reefs are eco-designed cylinders made from low-carbon concrete incorporating volcanic material, University of Galway explained. They are placed individually on the seabed to form a single connected structure covering an area of about half a hectare. Each 3D-printed structure is around 80cm high and 100cm in diameter and weighs around 600kg. It provides physical microhabitats such as tunnels, ledges and overhangs for corals and other marine life to colonise. “The early results are very encouraging, and if we can show that these approaches work, it could open up new possibilities to scale up the restoration of damaged coral reefs elsewhere in the Atlantic and beyond,” said Grehan. The scientists are using different approaches to bring the seabed back to life as part of the research. Some of the seabeds being tested are equipped with oyster shells, ceramic plates and tiles to encourage coral larvae to settle naturally, while others were fitted with fragments of living coral. These live coral fragments were collected from the ocean at a similar depth to the artificial ones and kept in seawater tanks under conditions designed to replicate their natural environment. Small branches were then taken from the colonies and attached to oyster shells before being anchored on the artificial reefs. The researchers found that the fragments placed on artificial reefs last year are alive and have been colonised by a range of marine species, including sea urchins, fish and crabs. Both restoration sites are protected from any further damage by bottom trawling, but that is not enough, said Marie-Claire Fabri, a research engineer in deep-sea benthic ecology at Ifremer and the co-leader of the Bay of Biscay region expedition, who called for active restoration efforts. “The ban on bottom trawling has helped stop the direct pressure on these habitats, but to go further – given the slow growth of these corals in the Atlantic – just a few millimetres per year – we cannot simply wait for them to recover on their own.” Scientists will continue to monitor these sites over the coming years, and work on better understanding their exact marine conditions to help better replicate the lost habitats. Redress brings 27 partner organisations from 15 countries to investigate the restoration of damaged deep-sea habitats across Europe. The EU-funded project also has similar artificial reef modules in restoration sites across the Atlantic and the Mediterranean. Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Are internships a stepping stone to future careers the way degrees are?
2026-09-21

Are internships a stepping stone to future careers the way degrees are?

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
Matthew J Mayhew, Courtney DeRoche and Yun-Han Weng of Ohio State University discuss whether an internship or a degree is the correct choice for career growth. A version of this article was originally published by The Conversation (CC BY-ND 4.0) College students are facing one of the most precarious labour markets in years. A recent New York Federal Reserve study pointed to a sharp increase in the post-college unemployed compared with other workers, with explanations ranging from artificial intelligence job displacement to a broader slowdown in hiring. One response has been a boom in trade-school and apprenticeship programmes that launch young people into ‘AI-proof’ careers. But students at community and four-year colleges can also beat these headwinds and launch a career through on-the-job internships. We’re a group of multidisciplinary scholars at The Ohio State University who study two paid internship programmes in science, technology, engineering and math, or STEM, with a proven record of landing jobs for students. Although the two initiatives use different models, they both illustrate four lessons that colleges and employers can use to turn internships into genuine career pathways. Just as notable is that a large share of these students come from diverse and underrepresented backgrounds, with many being the first in their families to go to college. These programmes can serve as a powerful equaliser for young people who may not otherwise have such career opportunities. Internships remain one of higher education’s most powerful tools for preparing students for work. Decades of research show that students who complete high-quality internships are more likely to secure job interviews and high-earning positions post-graduation. At the same time, the number of internship opportunities is diminishing, even though that preparation matters more than ever. Artificial intelligence is transforming entry-level work, leading employers to increasingly value the human skills that machines cannot easily replicate: judgement, adaptability, collaboration and the ability to solve unfamiliar problems. Academic leaders are recognising these pressures. More than one-third of university presidents and two-thirds of community college presidents told an Inside Higher Ed survey that they plan to add or expand apprenticeship pathways over the next three years. But it’s not just the number of internships that matters – internship quality counts just as much. Our research at the College Impact Laboratory examined learning through experience with two multi-institutional studies. One is Bridging Academic Training Through Experiential Research and Innovation, or BATTERI, led by principal investigator Jay Sayre and project manager Joanna Gardner. The other is Drive to Thrive. Across both initiatives, students benefited when their experiences combined substantive work with mentoring and professional development, rather than being treated as inexpensive labour. This distinction suggests that merely placing students in workplaces isn’t enough. The structure and quality of those experiences help determine whether internships lead to meaningful, high-earning careers. Drive to Thrive deploys an approach called ‘earn-as-you-learn’. Through partnerships with Ford dealerships, students alternate between classroom instruction and paid workplace experience. They work alongside experienced technicians, diagnose and repair customers’ vehicles and receive regular coaching. In this sense, it’s similar to the European model of traineeships for young people. This initiative reached 635 students at 18 community colleges, including 345 first-generation students and 300 students of colour. Through interviews and focus groups, a majority told us the internship helped them see themselves as true professionals. And several said the internship confirmed they had chosen the right career. The numbers also backed up our approach. Students in the earn-as-you-learn programme were almost 50pc more likely to graduate compared with students in a matched comparison group. Our other programme brings together clean energy manufacturing students from three different types of institutions – a major research university, a historically Black university and a community college – for a 10-week internship. The programme combines workplace learning with professional development workshops, peer networking, community engagement and projects with industry partners such as Honda, Stellantis and Schaeffler. This initiative took a distinctive approach by assigning multiple mentors to each student. The idea is to help students develop relationships with faculty members, technical experts, employers and classmates. This network, in turn, strengthened students’ confidence, professional identity and sense of belonging while complementing the technical skills they develop. To date, the formula has worked. More than half the participants in 2025 have already lined up either a new internship or job interviews. Industry partners also plan to rehire several members of the 2026 cohort as interns, extending their training beyond our programme and opening pathways into clean-tech careers. Join a culture that offers a world of possibilities Putting the closet in the cloud is a complex challenge, one that attracts people with an entrepreneurial spirit. Unlocking the potential of biology for patients Based on this record, we believe that colleges should build broader support networks around internships rather than expect a single supervisor to shoulder the entire responsibility for a student’s professional development. In both programmes, four valuable principles emerged. First is the importance of meaningful work. We believe interns should tackle real organisational challenges with deliverables that matter beyond the classroom. For example, two interns spent the summer addressing a safety challenge for Honda – how to prevent battery cells from catching fire and cooling them down when they do. After developing potential solutions, they presented their work to industry representatives. A senior engineer from the Honda Research Institute was so impressed that he contacted them about a post-internship opportunity. That sense of consequence changed how the students approached the work. Rather than viewing it as an assignment to complete, they were making a contribution that others depended on. Second is the constellation of support for students. The liaison between the institution and industry partners is essential, serving as the connective tissue that holds the internship together and ensuring that students don’t fall through the cracks. But that liaison alone isn’t enough. Students also need access to faculty, industry supervisors, technical experts and peers, since each brings a different kind of guidance. One strength of the clean energy programme, for example, is its inclusion of industry experts across institutions and organisations. Students benefit most when they can draw on multiple mentors and peer support to develop key collaborative skills. Third, students should learn how to normalise struggle. Internships aren’t a test of whether students can already function as professionals. They’re environments for students to try, fail and retry. When educators emphasise struggle as part of the learning process, students are encouraged to avoid perfectionism and instead ask for help when difficulties arise. For example, students submit weekly reports during the clean energy programme that give them the chance to discuss challenges, troubleshoot problems together and recognise that uncertainty is part of becoming a professional. Finally, it’s vital to make interns’ contributions visible. Presentations, project showcases and recognition from industry professionals reinforce that students’ work matters while strengthening their professional identities. We believe universities and industries alike should heed these lessons, which extend well beyond clean energy manufacturing and automotive technology. Successful programs build ecosystems that often determine whether students simply complete an internship or launch a career. As colleges respond to changing labour markets, they should resist measuring success just by how many internships they create. Instead, they should ask whether those opportunities help students become confident, capable professionals. Internship quantity opens the door to better outcomes after college. Internship quality changes lives. By Matthew J Mayhew, Courtney DeRoche and Yun-Han Weng Prof Matthew J Mayhew is a professor of higher education at The Ohio State University. He is interested in how collegiate conditions, educational practices and student experiences influence learning and democratic outcomes, including: moral reasoning; pluralism; productive exchange across worldview differences; and innovation capacity. To support the study of college and its impact on student development and learning, Mayhew has been awarded more than $16m in funding from sources including but not limited to the US Department of Education, the Ewing Marion Kauffman Foundation, the Andrew C Mellon Foundation, the Fetzer Institute and the Merrifield Family Foundation. Dr Courtney DeRoche is a postdoctoral researcher at The Ohio State University’s College Impact Laboratory, where she leads the ‘Drive to Thrive: Student Success in Career and Technical STEM’ project. Yun-Han Weng is a postdoctoral researcher at The Ohio State University. Her research focuses on collegiate environments, STEM education and internationalisation. She has been in the field for 10 years and has studied how different interventions and different collegiate climates impact students’ sense of belonging and learning outcomes. Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Sign up for the Daily Brief, Silicon Republic’s digest of need-to-know sci-tech news. Related: The Conversation, working life, Opinion, skills, higher education, Guest Column, internships, education, research

Google fined €403m by DPC over GDPR violations
2026-09-21

Google fined €403m by DPC over GDPR violations

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
Users could have been unaware that their data was being used for ads, said DPC deputy commissioner Graham Doyle. Ireland’s data protection watchdog has fined Google €403m and ordered the tech giant to become compliant with the region’s privacy regulations within six months. The Data Protection Commission (DPC), concluding a six-year-long probe, found that Google did not comply with the EU’s GDPR when processing data generated in the bloc. Its inquiry, triggered by complaints from European consumer rights organisations, including from BEUC, probed how the company processed location data across its ‘web & app activity’, ‘location history’ and ‘location accuracy’ features between May 2018 and February 2020. ‘Web & app activity’ is a Google account setting that processes information related to users’ activity on Google services, including sites and apps, meanwhile ‘location history’ tracks users’ locations and processes the data as visited places, activities and paths between locations. The complaints raised concerns relating to the “legality of Google’s processing of location data and the transparency surrounding that processing”, the DPC said in a statement announcing the probe in 2020. The probe found that Google infringed the GDPR by failing to lawfully process its location data, failing to demonstrate its compliance with the GDPR and failing to meet its transparency obligations across the features in question. “Location data can bring both benefits and harms to individuals,” said DPC deputy commissioner Graham Doyle. “It can greatly enhance the utility of online services, but it can also reveal a significant amount of information about an individual, including information that is inherently private. “The GDPR provides a high level of protection of personal data throughout the EEA, and requires that the processing of personal data must be carried out in a lawful, fair and transparent manner. “As a result of Google’s failures in this regard, individuals could have been unaware that their location was being used to, for example, influence them with ads or to infer their interests, and could lose control over their personal data. The retention of users’ location data for longer than necessary aggravated this loss of control.” In a statement to SiliconRepublic.com, a Google spokesperson said that the case centres around “historical policies” that have been updated since. “From 2019 onwards, we’ve significantly evolved our practices and launched robust tools that make managing location data simple,” the company said. In 2019, Google introduced auto-delete controls that allow users to automatically delete data on a rolling three-, 18- or 36-month basis. In 2023, it updated its location history feature to enable timelines to be stored directly on users’ devices. Google also said it does not store precise location data in ‘web & app activity’ and allows users to manage how their data is used for ads. This is the DPC’s fourth largest fine since the GDPR came into effect. In 2023, Meta received the largest fine issued by the watchdog to date, raking in €1.2bn in penalties after illegally transferring Facebook user data from the EU to the US. Last year, TikTok was fined €530m for transferring personal user data to China, while Meta was also subject to a separate €405m fine in 2022 for publicly disclosing contact details of children using Instagram’s business accounts. Meta Now Banning Ads That Promote Feminism
2026-09-21

Meta Now Banning Ads That Promote Feminism

Futurism | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
For years, human rights monitors have been outraged at the selective policing of content on Meta’s social media platforms like Facebook and Instagram. The company’s latest enforcement action is hardly shocking at this point, but it provides a crystal clear example of the kinds of things Meta will — and won’t — allow on its apps. In a bizarre case of digital censorship, Meta’s content moderation system stepped in to stop a local theatre in Barcelona from promoting a stage adaptation on the grounds that it violated a rule on promoting “social topics,” per the Guardian. The production in question is an adaptation of “A Room of One’s Own,” a book by the legendary English author and feminist Virginia Woolf. According to the Teatre Raval, planned promotional posts about the show were blocked on both Instagram and Facebook, likely by an automated content moderation system. After reaching out to Meta about why the promo campaign had been stomped out, the company replied that “any mention of civil rights, feminism or social reform are usually classified as ‘social topics’ by our automatic system,” and therefore rejected. According to the Guardian, the company then cryptically recommended the Raval remove “key words” related to social activism, so the promo campaign can pass the automated censor — though the company failed to explain what, exactly, its content system objected to. “We don’t know if the publicity has been rejected because of the name of Virginia Woolf or because of the word feminism, or both,” Clara Sanchis, who stars in the one-woman show told the Spanish newspaper La Vanguardia, per the Guardian, which translated the comments. “In any case, it’s a way of erasing from the map advances that have taken centuries to achieve,” Sanchis continued. “We have given social media unlimited power and every day we can see how dangerous this is. It’s bias and manipulation.” The automated rejection and the ensuing apathy from Meta come after some galling incidents where Meta refused to take down content significantly more radical than a theatrical performance about a literary figure whose books are read by high schoolers. In August, Futurism reported that Meta was refusing to take meaningful action against a far-right influencer campaign to drum up support for a “crusader march on Dearborn” Michigan, organized by self-described “anti-Arab” extremist Jake Lang. Though Lang’s initial post wasn’t “sponsored,” meaning he didn’t officially pay to promote his call-to-action as the Teatre Raval was trying to do, his post nonetheless garnered thousands of reactions, comments, and shares from his 274,000 followers. It also spawned a deluge of copycat posts extolling racism and Islamophobia, some of which contained explicit calls for violence which Meta refused to take down. And earlier in September, Meta was found to be hosting a massive number of AI-generated videos depicting horrific acts of child abuse. Meta removed a tiny handful of videos after Futurism approached the company for comment — but refused to act on others, leaving the vast majority of violent AI Reels untouched. In all, Meta has drawn a revolting line in the sand — with nominally progressive “social topics” on the verboten side, and base depictions of torture and calls for violence allowed sight unseen. Hundreds of Flights Grounded Across the Eastern Seaboard as Government Rolls Out AI Air Traffic Control System
2026-09-21

Hundreds of Flights Grounded Across the Eastern Seaboard as Government Rolls Out AI Air Traffic Control System

Futurism | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
Hundreds of flights across New Jersey, Philadelphia, New York City, and Boston were delayed Monday afternoon after the US Federal Aviation Administration issued a massive ground stop impacting thousands of travelers — right as the government starts testing an AI air traffic control system, though there’s no current indication that the two incidents are connected. According to early reporting by Reuters, over 600 flights were delayed across Newark and Philadelphia alone, in addition to hundreds more across four other east coast airports. Dozens of imminent takeoffs were likewise aborted across the busy flight corridor when the ground stops — which appeared to roll northward from Philadelphia — were issued. Preliminary reports indicate some sort of air traffic control glitch at one of the major airports along the East Coast, with Bloomberg TV reporting a circuit malfunction at an air traffic control facility in Philadelphia, and Reuters noting that a fiber optics cable was accidentally clipped in New Jersey. Meanwhile, the BBC reports that Boston’s Logan International Airport is warning travelers to expect extensive delays due to broad “equipment issues across the north-east.” As one FAA spokesperson told CBS21, the ground stop is due to “issues with some frequencies at Philadelphia TRACON,” also known as Terminal Radar Approach Control, the radar system that guides planes in and out of airports. At the time of writing, it isn’t exactly clear what’s behind the turmoil. What is clear is that the region’s air traffic control system is a tangled mess — and unfortunately, this could just be a sign of things to come. Strikingly, the massive air traffic outage comes right as the Trump administration moves ahead with a controversial AI air traffic control system known as “Strategic Management of Airspace, Routes and Trajectories,” or SMART. According to further reporting by Bloomberg, the federal government is “soft launching” the program this week across the Washington DC region, where it’s now dolling out AI-generated recommendations to flight traffic personnel. As part of the rollout, SMART will reportedly be installed in a number of East Coast airports, like Reagan National Airport in Virginia, and Baltimore/Washington International Thurgood Marshall Airport in Maryland, though it’s not known which facilities have officially gone live with the AI tool. Any connection between the massive ground stop and the SMART soft launch would be speculation at this point, and early reports don’t seem to indicate a connection between the two events. What is clear is that air traffic control is in dire straights across the United States — though the American public has yet to encounter any federal system which a hallucinating, error-prone AI assistant can’t make significantly worse. It Turns Out the US Military Really Did Use AI to Blow Up a School Full of Iranian Children

Futurism | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
Now over half a year into the United States’ war on Iran, it’s becoming abundantly clear that AI has been instrumental to the American campaign. On the home front, AI and its infrastructure is quietly shaping the ideological battlefield: President Donald Trump is spewing out wartime propaganda slopped together by generative AI, as US-owned data centers and tech company offices become widely accepted as military targets. AI is also proving instrumental for helping US Central Command choose targets for obliteration via drone strikes or missiles — so much so that an AI system played a pivotal role in perpetuating an American war crime. According to new reporting by Bloomberg, the Pentagon’s investigation into the February 28th air strikes on the Shajareh Tayyebeh girls’ school — a double-tap strike which killed over 150 people, including at least 123 children — concluded that the attack was the result of outdated intelligence and over-reliance on an AI targeting system. Interviewed by Bloomberg, anonymous military investigators involved in the probe characterized the horrifying attack as the result of a series of small “errors,” rather than one ill-fated mistake. Those errors, stemming from dusty intel, seem to have been compounded by the involvement of an as-yet unidentified AI targeting system, likely similar to the one used by American military officers to fast-track air strikes on over 2,000 Iranian targets within a four-day window in the early days of the conflict. The latest info directly refutes president Donald Trump’s own narrative around the deadly incident. In July, Trump downplayed the United States’ responsibility for the attack, even suggesting that images and footage of the massacre could have been generated by AI. Yet as a UN fact-finding commission wrote in its report last week, there are “reasonable grounds” to conclude the US attacks amount to “war crimes” — the girls’ school attack being just one prominent example. Whether the full Pentagon investigation will ever be made available to the public is unclear. What is certain is that killer AI is already here — and it doesn’t need to be sentient to help humans claim hundreds of innocent lives. Behold: Video of “First-Ever” Human Versus Robot MMA Fight
2026-09-21

Behold: Video of “First-Ever” Human Versus Robot MMA Fight

Futurism | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
We’ve seen humanoid bots pull off impressive martial arts choreographies, run faster than the world’s fastest person, tear it up on the dance floor, and face off in the ring against each other. But — at least beyond a few humans catching stray blows from robots in stunts gone awry — we’d been yet to see a robot contender square off in the ring against a homo sapien fighter. Until now, that is. A new video making the rounds on social media shows humanoid robot fighting company REK have one of its androids spar with influencer — and major TikTok personality — Frankie LaPenna, who’s known for, among other things, stuffing his shorts to make it look like he has a huge behind. (LaPenna is such a big deal that he recently appeared on NBC‘s “America’s Got Talent,” where it was ultimately determined that he did not.) “For the first time in history, a human faces off against a six-foot-tall robot,” an announcer exclaims in the video. “Dude, I’m scared for you man,” one of the match officials tells LaPenna with a laugh. The 44-second clip shows the heavily padded influencer take swipes at the imposing robot inside a plastic-lined octagon — but to no avail. The brief match has LaPenna getting repeatedly kicked in the chest and midsection, only to be thrown across the ring with one final kick, seemingly ending the fight. (The robot, in a theatrical twist, had been outfitted with a terrifying “Terminator”-like head.) While we can certainly see at least some entertainment value of having daring human athletes square off with machines, there’s clearly something off with the match. For one, LaPenna clearly struggles to land a proper blow, suggesting he may be pulling his punches and eventually throwing the fight. And whether the robot really has enough force to throw LaPenna across the entire ring with a simple kick is debatable at best. “Is this just a showcase or did that robot legit yeet that dude five meters across the ring?” one Reddit user wondered. “It’s still clumsy… for now,” another added ominously. Besides, the stocky, 28-year-old influencer is well known for playing the heel in public and is willing to throw himself the line of fire for comedic effect. He recently has shared videos of himself crashing a bike into an office desk, jousting while standing on a boat, and even playing a game of cards in the vicinity of a raging bull. In other words, the jury is still out on how an actual MMA fighter would fare against a six-foot robot. But considering what they’re going up against, we’d hazard a guess that it wouldn’t exactly be much of a fair fight. Signs of Crisis at Flock as It Offers Employees Buyouts to Quit and Get Off Payroll
2026-09-21

Signs of Crisis at Flock as It Offers Employees Buyouts to Quit and Get Off Payroll

Futurism | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
As backlash against its surveillance network refuses to die down, Flock Safety is kicking off an employee buyout program that’ll let workers leave the company with a “generous” severance package, Wired reports. It’s seemingly an admission that the company is in turmoil. Protestors have targeted Flock’s AI-powered license plate scanning cameras so fiercely that many cities and police departments are cancelling or declining to extend their contracts, which could leave the enterprise short of revenue goals, according to Wired‘s sources. It’s either buyouts for the 1,500 worker-strong company, or layoffs. Employees who want to leave are getting a solid deal, at least according to Flock. The voluntary severance program, which started accepting applications Friday, gives employees several months of health care coverage and the ability to exercise stock options within two years of separation. Some employees have reportedly said they’ve received buyout offers in the tens of thousands of dollars. Flock, in its professed munificence, called the buyout package as the “most generous” ever offered by the company, extolling that it provides “greater agency” for its employees. There’s no word yet on how many people have applied or will be accepted, but applicants are expected to hear a decision on October 9. Most of those who get accepted will leave by October 29. Sources familiar with the program said they expected that a significant number of employees will leave. It’s not just the overwhelming backlash against the company that could push employees to exit. There are also rumors that the company might sell off parts or all of its business. “When you see enough of the vandalism,” one of the people told Wired, “you know the writing is on the wall.” An analysis by the advocacy group Secure Justice found that at least 214 cities and counties have dropped their Flock contracts since 2021 — and 90 of those cancelled or lapsed contracts came in 2026 alone. In August, an average of three local administrations were cancelling their Flock contracts each day. Because of the purpose of Flock’s services — conducting mass surveillance — these cancellations have created a vicious cycle. The more municipalities that its cameras go down in, the less useful that Flock becomes to its remaining customers. Some police departments have explicitly cited the loss of coverage from neighboring towns cancelling their contracts as a reason to do the same. When Flock was confronted with the cancellation data less than a month ago, a spokesperson confidently responded that renewals were outpacing cancellations. But if the buyout program is any indication, there’s a lot more anxiety about its future behind the scenes. Bloomberg Finds That the AI Industry’s Finances Are a “Wobbly House of Cards”
2026-09-21

Bloomberg Finds That the AI Industry’s Finances Are a “Wobbly House of Cards”

Futurism | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
While the majority of AI leaders seem to agree that we need to take the foot off the AI development pedal or else risk disaster, Wall Street isn’t exactly enthralled by the idea. For years now, analysts have warned that the United States economy is massively overindexed on AI and resting on a staggering amount of debt, with enormous data center projects and eye-bulging capital expenditures fueling fears over a growing bubble. And now that most major frontier AI labs are looking to slow things down, investors are ringing the alarm over a “wobbly house of cards,” as Bloomberg reports. If this house of cards were to collapse, they warn, the consequences could be devastating. While it’s an already-familiar refrain at this point, it simply — and perhaps fortunately — hasn’t been tested just yet. But the longer we wait, the more dire the outcome, as the stakes continue to grow at an alarming pace. “People may not fully grasp just how wound up the market and the economy is in all of this,” Callodine Capital Management CEO Jim Morrow told Bloomberg. “There are just so many things to unravel if it starts.” According to the latest estimates, AI-related spending is currently accounting for roughly half of the United States’ GDP growth. Without it, the country could spiral into a financial crisis on the scale of the dot-com crash. Meanwhile, tech leaders, including Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei, his counterpart at OpenAI, Sam Altman, and even Elon Musk, argue that we need to slow down AI development in light of what they perceive to be an existential threat. But whether that’s even an option remains debatable, per Bloomberg. For one, the world’s other biggest AI economy, China, is laughing at at the very idea, dismissing the remarks as “fear-mongering.” “If we see AI development slow, that means capex is likely to slow,” Ameriprise chief market strategist Anthony Saglimbene told the magazine. “Any slowdown would reset the profit expectations for the entire ecosystem. Given how concentrated the market is to AI, that would be a severe headwind.” Rising interest rates could raise the stakes even further, making it even more expensive for companies to borrow money to cover their enormous projected expenditures. Then there’s the simple fact that nobody knows if any of this spending will pay off in the long run. A return on investment is little more than a distant and tentative dream in 2026 — and open source AI systems are now constantly nipping at the heels of frontier models, making a profitable business model feel hazier than ever. If the house of cards were to collapse, some companies would probably survive while countless others fold. “Will there be winners? Absolutely,” Boston Partners director of global market research Michael Mullaney told Bloomberg. “It’s hard to say who is going to wind up on the other side of this thing and coining money to justify all their expenses.” “There will be, but it’s not going to be a boatload of companies, it’s going to be a handful of companies,” he added. More on the AI bubble: Economists Concerned the AI Bubble Is About to Blow

Morphotonics raises €40M to expand its display tech into data centers
2026-09-22

Morphotonics raises €40M to expand its display tech into data centers

TechCrunch | 2026-09-22

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Disrupt ticket savings of up to $200 + 50% off a second ends Sept 25, 11:59 p.m. PT. REGISTER HERE. The Netherlands’ ASML is possibly the biggest name in lithography, using its machines to etch patterns onto silicon wafers inside a computer chip factory known as a fab. Fellow Dutch company Morphotonics is smaller and works in a different corner of the same field: nanoimprint lithography, or NIL, a process used to manufacture the displays in AR glasses and other optics. It said it has benefited from ASML’s supply chain and uses many of the same vendors. Morphotonics has spent 12 years developing that technology, largely under the radar. Now, as demand for smart glasses and other optics surges, the company said Tuesday it has raised €40 million to scale up production and push into a new market: optical components for data centers.The round was backed by 3M Ventures, Innovation Industries, BOM, and Invest-NL. The European Innovation Council (EIC) Fund; Ernij Next, a Dutch family office; and the European Investment Bank (EIB) also participated. Morphotonics’ NIL work means the startup creates a “stamp” and applies it to photosensitive materials to create various kinds of displays, from augmented reality glasses to privacy screens for cars. This is useful in creating the waveguide technology used in smart glasses, including the Meta Ray-Ban Display, Even Reality, and Magic Leap. Waveguides are thin pieces of glass or plastic that deflect light along a specific path so that they display an image in front of the wearer’s eyes. Smart glasses are just one application of Morphotonics’ technology, but it’s currently the most in-demand one. This week, China said sales of smart glasses in the country doubled in the first eight months of this year. In June, the market research firm IDC noted that shipments of smart glasses with displays are expected to reach 12.2 million by 2030. As that market grows, manufacturers are looking for machines like Morphotonics’ that can produce those displays at scale. The latest fundraising is part of an extended round that began in 2024, raised in multiple installments. The company has used the money to more than double its headcount to 60 employees, up from roughly 30 in September 2024, when CEO Hugo Da Silva joined. Morphotonics still plans to hire and expects headcount to stabilize around 70 to 75 people. “Typically, display manufacturers would acquire our equipment as part of the supply chain, and we integrate it into the entire manufacturing process. We teach them the process, we provide the chemicals, and they can manufacture that,” Da Silva said. The company’s manufacturing is heavily concentrated in Asia, with teams in China, Taiwan, South Korea, and the U.S. “Strong local presence is very important,” he said. Da Silva said the company’s next machine, currently being built, will be capable of producing more than 6 million waveguides a year. He said it’s expected to start shipping early next year. Beyond displays, that expansion also includes a push into co-packaged optics, a technology used in data centers that use Photonic Integrated Circuits — chips that use light instead of electricity — to move data between servers and network equipment. The startup hasn’t shipped any machines in this area, but it said customers have validated its tech. The company sells both hardware machines and the process through licensing to ensure that customers get the best results. Right now, roughly 90% of the startup’s revenue comes from hardware sales, with 10 to 15 systems deployed globally. The company expects to reach 50 deployments in the next two to three years, which should also increase its revenue from services. The man who built Apple’s stores doesn’t buy Silicon Valley’s bet on AI shopping
2026-09-21

The man who built Apple’s stores doesn’t buy Silicon Valley’s bet on AI shopping

TechCrunch | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Disrupt ticket savings of up to $200 + 50% off a second ends Sept 25, 11:59 p.m. PT. REGISTER HERE. While Silicon Valley is spending billions to make AI agents shop for us, the man who pioneered Apple’s retail stores thinks the industry is overestimating how much of shopping people will hand over to machines — and believes physical stores will continue to thrive. Ron Johnson, 66, has heard predictions about the demise of physical retail before. He joined Apple in 2000 to build its retail business as online shopping was beginning to take off, and went on to help create a store network that became central to how Apple sells its products and connects with customers. “AI is a new technology that will improve the online shopping experience,” Johnson said in an interview. “But I don’t know that it’s going to change which way we shop.” Johnson’s view comes as some of the world’s biggest tech companies are trying to push AI deeper into shopping, betting that agents can automate more of how we find and buy items — a concept commonly called agentic commerce. Google is making that push with its Universal Commerce Protocol, a standard designed to help AI agents take consumers from product discovery to checkout. OpenAI, meanwhile, is turning ChatGPT into a shopping destination where users can research, compare, and, in some cases, even buy products without leaving the chatbot. Asked whether he could imagine someone letting an agent choose and buy a $1,000 or $2,000 laptop without ever visiting a website or store, Johnson was unequivocal: “Honestly, nobody’s going to do that.” Buying a laptop, he argued, is too personal a purchase to simply delegate to an AI agent. Buyers want to feel its weight, see the display and decide which size works for them. AI may narrow the choices, Johnson told TechCrunch. He added that many consumers would still want to experience the product themselves before spending that kind of money. “AI will never be able to have you physically experience a product,” Johnson said. Instead, he expects agents to better inform consumers before they walk into a store. “They’ll just become more informed shoppers when they come to the store.” Johnson’s conviction stems from a decision Apple made more than two decades ago. Apple’s physical stores, he said, were designed not only as places to buy Macs, but also as places where people could try products, learn how to use them, and return for help when something went wrong. Some of those bets are revisited in Shop Different: How Retail Revealed Apple’s Genius, Johnson’s new book about building Apple’s retail operations alongside Steve Jobs. Competitors, he said, borrowed Apple’s glass-heavy design, open layouts, and even versions of the Genius Bar, but often missed the people. “The secret sauce for Apple has always been its people, the people in the store, and how they treat the customer,” Johnson said. Apple Store employees are not paid on commission, Johnson said, unlike the sales culture common across much of retail. The idea, he said, was to remove the pressure to sell and instead have employees figure out what a customer actually needed. After leaving Apple, Johnson took over J.C. Penney in 2011 with an ambitious plan to reinvent the struggling department-store chain. He was ousted less than two years later after sales plunged. Johnson now says he tried to change too much, too quickly, without bringing employees and customers along. Apple’s stores had effectively been a startup that evolved alongside the company’s products. J.C. Penney, on the other hand, was a turnaround that required a different approach. “I applied a startup mentality to what needed to be a turnaround transformation,” he recalled. Johnson later returned to the startup world, founding Enjoy Technology, an e-commerce company that brought technology products and setup services directly to customers’ homes. The startup filed for bankruptcy in 2022 and sold substantially all of its assets to Asurion. For all his skepticism about how AI will reshape shopping, Johnson remains bullish on the technology itself. “I’m a real believer in AI. I’m an AI optimist,” he said. He believes Jobs would have embraced AI, too, but not as a substitute for human judgment. “There’s no substitute for human intuition,” Johnson said, recalling Jobs’ belief in bringing smart people together to debate problems and find new ways of looking at them. OpenAI forms math advisory group as its AI resolves more than 100 open problems
2026-09-21

OpenAI forms math advisory group as its AI resolves more than 100 open problems

TechCrunch | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Disrupt ticket savings of up to $200 + 50% off a second ends Sept 25, 11:59 p.m. PT. REGISTER HERE. On Monday, OpenAI announced a new independent advisory group hosted at the Institute for Advanced Study in Princeton, New Jersey. Called the Advisory Group on Mathematics and Artificial Intelligence, the group is meant to give mathematicians more input into the company’s math-oriented research. “This group will serve as a bridge to the mathematical community and broader public, giving mathematicians a voice in how we move forward,” the post reads. The announcement comes after the abrupt publication of a solution to the Navier-Stokes Millennium Prize problem. As part of the announcement of the new group, OpenAI also claims that the same internal model has resolved more than 100 additional open problems across most areas of mathematics. Many prominent mathematicians have taken issue with the frenzied pace of those results. Earlier this month, 25 Fields Medal-winning mathematicians signed an open letter arguing that AI labs are threatening their intellectual work as they seek to one-up each other with solutions to famous math problems. True to its name, OpenAI’s new group will mostly serve in an advisory capacity, assessing the significance of new results and coordinating their release. While members won’t be paid, they can offer unsolicited advice, go public with their views, and control their own membership, giving them a measure of independence from the company. However, the group won’t be given leeway to slow down or redirect OpenAI’s ongoing mathematical research. As the blog post puts it, “the group will not be responsible for advising us on how to pace our internal progress on mathematics.” The Institute for Advanced Study emphasized this point in its own announcement. “Although we will give advice, we do not have decision making power at any AI company, and the responsibility for the decisions made by any company will rest with that company,” said the institute in a press release. Nine prominent mathematicians have been named as initial members of the group. Notably, only one of the members — IAS’s Camillo De Lellis — also signed the Fields Medalists’ letter. Meta’s Muse is outpacing ChatGPT’s early mobile launch
2026-09-21

Meta’s Muse is outpacing ChatGPT’s early mobile launch

TechCrunch | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Disrupt ticket savings of up to $200 + 50% off a second ends Sept 25, 11:59 p.m. PT. REGISTER HERE. Meta’s latest AI app, Muse, could be the social giant’s next major hit, new data suggests. According to just- released estimates from market intelligence provider Apptopia, Muse’s app on mobile devices has been downloaded more times during its first 12 days on the market than ChatGPT was in the 12 days after its debut. (This data compares the U.S. and Canada markets only.) Muse also has more daily active users than ChatGPT had at the time, the firm found. Until now, it had been difficult to make an exact apples-to-apples comparison between these two apps, given that they had pursued different launch strategies. When ChatGPT arrived on mobile, it was made available globally, but only on iOS. Muse, meanwhile, is available across both Apple’s App Store and Google Play, but only in the U.S. and Canada. To better align the numbers for comparison, Apptopia looked only at the iOS data for the U.S. and Canada for both apps during the first 12 days of their respective launches. In this subset of the data, Muse has now seen 1.8 million downloads to ChatGPT’s 1.3 million. Overall, Muse has seen 2.8 million total installs globally in its first 12 days, the firm also said. Its growth hasn’t yet stagnated, either; Muse has moved up from its original position as No. 2 overall on the U.S. App Store immediately after its launch to now No. 1, as Business Insider reported on Friday. That jump put the app higher than ChatGPT, the outlet noted. Another firm, Appfigures, said at the time that Muse had then crossed 1 million downloads. In addition, Apptopia’s data indicates that Muse’s U.S. daily active users are now higher than they were for ChatGPT at the same point after its launch. When comparing just the U.S. mobile app daily active users, Muse comes in higher with 642,000 daily users compared with 231,000 for ChatGPT at the time. To be fair to the fact that Muse is available across both iOS and Android, while ChatGPT launched on iOS only, Apptopia narrowed the comparison to iOS alone. Yet, even here, Muse is coming in higher, with 359,000 daily active users on iOS, still above ChatGPT’s figures from that time period. Apptopia can only provide third-party estimates about an app’s downloads and active users; it doesn’t have direct access to Meta’s internal figures. But even if these numbers are only correct from a general “ballpark” perspective, they indicate that Muse could have a shot at becoming Meta’s newest top app. Meta already perfected its cross-promotion strategy when it launched Instagram’s Threads, which now has 500+ million users, thanks to heavy marketing and integrations in Meta’s largest apps, including Instagram and Facebook. Muse is likely to get a similar push, given that the AI app can connect with both of those platforms. It also works inside WhatsApp, so that could give it another boost. While Apptopia doesn’t have visibility into Meta’s cross-promotion efforts or ads directly, it did note that over 95% of Muse’s users are also Facebook users and 63% are Instagram users. Meta, which has been asked for comment, has not yet shared public figures related to Muse’s early adoption. Kairos Power gets up to $100M from Samsung group to build nuclear reactor for Google
2026-09-21

Kairos Power gets up to $100M from Samsung group to build nuclear reactor for Google

TechCrunch | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Disrupt ticket savings of up to $200 + 50% off a second ends Sept 25, 11:59 p.m. PT. REGISTER HERE. Kairos Power has picked engineering firm Samsung C&T to help build its 50-megawatt demonstration reactor for Google, which the nuclear startup is aiming to have completed by 2030. As part of the deal, Samsung C&T will make an equity investment in Kairos and provide “in-kind” engineering services, the combination of which is worth up to $100 million, Kairos Power said Monday. The equity investment will make up $70 million of the total, a Kairos spokesperson told TechCrunch. Samsung C&T has built or helped build about a dozen nuclear reactors around the globe. Kairos is part of a wave of nuclear startups that have benefited from the surge in power demand from AI data centers. Google first signed a deal with the company in fall 2024, calling on Kairos to build nuclear reactors capable of generating about half a gigawatt of electricity by 2035. Currently, Kairos is building two reactors in Oak Ridge, Tennessee. The first, Hermes 1, is a low-power demonstrator that will help the company refine its commercial design. The second, Hermes 2, is Kairos’ first commercial-scale reactor. Output from Hermes will count as the first 50 megawatts of its deal with Google. The startup received approval from the Nuclear Regulatory Commission in late 2024 to build the reactors. It expects Hermes 2 to begin operating in 2030, in line with its original agreement with Google. Hermes 2 will be a fluoride salt-cooled high-temperature reactor, a design that has been floated for years but never built at commercial scale. Fluoride salts have high boiling points, which will help keep pressure levels low. That, in turn, will limit the risk of high-pressure blowouts in case of any parts failures. Kairos is also using a relatively new form of nuclear fuel known as TRISO, which encapsulates tiny seeds of uranium fuel in layers of ceramic and carbon, which are then loaded into billiard ball-sized spheres. The arrangement is intended to prevent meltdowns. The startup has about five years to get its first power plant up and running, which is an accelerated but manageable timeline in the nuclear industry. To fulfill the terms of its deal with Google, Kairos will need to move even faster on subsequent reactors, something Samsung C&T might be able to help with. ‘Healthy and Delicious Can Coexist’: Experts Discuss Making Food More Joyful and Sustainable
2026-09-22

‘Healthy and Delicious Can Coexist’: Experts Discuss Making Food More Joyful and Sustainable

TIME | 2026-09-22

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings At a TIME100 Talks event in New York City on Sept. 21, chefs and food industry experts discussed the challenges our communities face in making food healthier, more accessible, and more joyful for everyone. Cecile Beliot-Zind, the CEO of Bel Group, which sponsored the event, said that her company believes “there is a model to make healthier and sustainable food for all possible,” but one of the issues facing the industry today is that people “keep on opposing instead of combining” ideas. “We believe that the right way to define healthier food is to combine what usually people tend to see as trade-offs,” she said. “The big issue of our industry: If you want to scale healthier and sustainable food, you are first to make sure and to believe very strongly that health can be combined with taste, that nutrition can be combined with accessibility, that fair revenues for the farmers—who, by the way, feed all of us—could be combined with fair values for the communities we feed.” She gave an example: Bel Group makes Babybel, the popular small cheese snack wrapped in a red wax coating, which Beliot-Zind called “the perfect snacks.” “The reason why kids and I love Babybel is because it’s convenient, because it’s a great natural source of protein, because I play with the wax. It gives me joy,” she said. Bel Group, she continued, focuses on balancing these two ideas: making food both healthy and desirable. “Healthy food will have an impact on the world if we are capable to make it nutritious enough to matter, accessible enough to be everywhere for everyone, and desirable enough to be chosen,” she said. “The issue with the food industry is that the big food manufacturers have created desire for product[s] that provide empty calories. They scale it, they spread it all over the world. We feed the world with fat and sugar at a low cost. … It hurts the planet, and it hurts the health of the people.” TIME executive editor and panel moderator Nikhil Kumar then asked Vikas Khanna, a chef, author, and filmmaker, how he navigates making healthy food as a restaurateur. Khanna, who was on the 2026 TIME100 list, talked about how, at his New York City restaurant Bungalow, he and his team have worked to make their food delicious, healthy, and sustainable. “There’s also an extreme stigma around ethnic cuisines not being healthy,” Khanna said, but it isn’t necessarily accurate. Much of Indian cuisine, he said, “is based on the principles of Ayurveda and sustainability.” And eating healthy doesn’t have to come at the cost of authenticity. For instance, Bungalow has reduced the ratio of creams used in its foods. “I do understand you adding 40% cream makes everything taste more delicious,” he admitted, “but I want people to feel as if they’re eating at home. I have never met an Indian who says that we use cream in our home cooking.” Marcus Samuelsson, a chef and author, spoke about how there’s been a long-held belief that  healthy food can’t be tasty—and how it’s the role of a chef to disprove that idea. “Healthy and delicious can coexist. Healthy and delicious definitely can be celebrated together,” Samuelsson said. “We think about healthy and delicious constantly. And it’s not either or.” He went on to urge everyone to learn how to cook: “Learn how to cook, and with that, you will follow your own curiosity and you will feel better. Guaranteed.” Ertharin Cousin, the CEO and managing director of Food Systems for the Future, said that, while she thinks it’s a great idea for everyone to learn how to cook their own food, it can be a challenging task for people from low-income communities who may not have access to regular refrigeration or electricity or gas to support cooking. “When we think about making that nutritious plate more available, we also need to think about how to support the households without access to the cooking and storage that we take for granted,” Cousin said. “Everyone wants to put a plate in front of their child that is going to ensure that that child thrives. Not everyone has the capacity to do it from a financial standpoint or from the tools that are required to make it available.” Cousin also spoke about how “we grow enough calories,” but not enough food “to support the nutritious, diverse diet that’s necessary for the body to thrive.” That has led to nutritious and diverse diets becoming unaffordable for many Americans, and for many people around the world, which results in malnutrition, according to Cousin. At the same time, she said, “we have made food that is less nutrient-dense more affordable and available,” which has led to an overconsumption of unhealthy foods. She went on to say that it should be the responsibility of everyone—ad companies, food processors, retailers, policymakers, and even consumers—to make changes across the entire system to make our food healthier. The panelists ended the discussion by emphasizing the idea that food should be both joyful and healthy—that the two are not mutually exclusive. Cousin even said that “there’s a place for indulgent food,” though it shouldn’t be “at the center of your plate.” “Food is joy. It’s health. It’s family. It’s culture. It’s love. You think about what moms go through to put food on the table for their families every night and how they think about what they’re providing for their children, for their futures. And we’ve lost some of that,” Cousin said. “We need to ensure that they have access to that diverse diet that is required for them to thrive—for the child to thrive.” TIME100 Talks: Architecting Healthier Food Systems for All was presented by Bel Group.

Trump and Iran Exchange Threats as Leaders Converge for Key U.N. Gathering. Can the Meeting Cool Rising Tensions?

TIME | 2026-09-21

Update your preferences in Account Settings Law enforcement secures an entrance to the United Nations during the start of the General Assembly in New York City on September 21, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Delegations from the U.S. and Iran are traveling to New York for the world’s largest diplomatic gathering this week in what experts say could be a key moment for determining the direction of the Iran war nearly seven months in. Tensions between the warring countries are mounting ahead of the United Nations General Assembly (UNGA), with Iranian leaders and President Donald Trump exchanging a fresh round of threats. Trump on Sunday weighed whether he should “blow the entire nation up” in an interview with Fox News, warning that Iran “better behave” and saying that “very big things” would happen in the war, without offering more detail. According to the outlet, Trump described his options as “wiping Iran out, letting them rot economically, or making a deal.” A spokesperson for Iran’s Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC), Hossein Mohebbi, responded with a warning that if the U.S. were to launch fresh attacks against Iran, Tehran would use “new weapons and capabilities,” according to Iran's Fars news agency. “If a new aggression takes place, we will definitely change the weapons and geography of the war,” Mohebbi said. The threats come after active hostilities in the conflict resumed in the last month when the U.S. and Iran traded fresh strikes amid tensions over the Strait of Hormuz, a key waterway in the global oil market. Meaningful negotiations between the two countries have meanwhile been stalled for weeks following the breakdown of a memorandum of understanding leaders signed in June. And Yemen’s Iranian-backed Houthi rebels have further shaken the Gulf region by launching attacks against Saudi Arabia and capturing Perim Island, a strategic foothold in the Bab El-Mandeb Strait, another vital maritime trade route in the region. Read More: What Is the Status of the U.S.-Iran Peace Talks? Here's What Both Sides Are Saying Experts tell TIME that the U.N. General Assembly gathering, where delegations from Iran, the U.S., and key Gulf states will be in attendance, could offer a critical chance to deescalate the situation and attempt to revive peace efforts—although making progress in negotiations could be an uphill battle. “This week in New York, there is an opportunity, especially for the mediating countries, to try to prevent tensions from once again spiraling, and potentially this time spiraling out of control,” says Ali Vaez, the deputy program director for the Middle East and North Africa at the International Crisis Group. “It's the last best opportunity to try to prevent an escalation either in October or in November.” Top officials from the U.S. and Iran will be present at the U.N. meeting this week, including Trump, Secretary of State Marco Rubio, and U.S. ambassador to the U.N. Mike Waltz. Iranian President Masoud Pezeshkian and Foreign Minister Abbas Araghchi will be in attendance as part of a slimmed-down delegation from the country. Both Trump and Pezeshkian are set to deliver addresses to the assembly. Vaez says, however, that any effort to revive peace talks between the two countries during the event will largely rely on mediation by Gulf states. Trump is set to hold talks with leaders from the Gulf Cooperation Council—a regional alliance including Saudi Arabia, the United Arab Emirates, Qatar, Kuwait, Oman, and Bahrain—on the sidelines of the gathering in the coming days. Sources with knowledge of the anticipated meeting told Axios, which first reported the news, that discussions are expected to concern next steps in the war with Iran, with a focus on U.S. ideas for a strategy after the conflict concludes. Vaez believes that the Qataris could play a leading role in mediation efforts, with the support of countries such as Saudi Arabia, Oman, Turkey, Pakistan, and Egypt. “It all depends on whether the mediating countries would be able to find a mutually acceptable formula,” he says of the possibility of the U.S. and Iran coming to some kind of agreement. “Because at this moment, both sides are in a zero-sum situation in which there is literally no overlap between what the Iranians demand and what the U.S. is seeking to achieve.” Given what he characterized as maximalist demands from both the U.S. and Iran, Vaez contends that “some sort of a sweetener,” or incentive, must be given to each side for a deal to be reached, which he says may come in the form of revisions to the now-expired memorandum of understanding. Sanam Valki, director of the Middle East and North Africa Programme at the policy institute Chatham House, agrees that it is far more likely that Gulf states mediate between Iran and the U.S. But, she adds, a direct exchange between U.S. and Iranian delegations is also a possibility—albeit a very slim one. “The big question out there is: Will there be some sort of surprise encounter between the Iranians and the U.S.?” Valki says. Trump told Fox News’ Trey Yingst on Sunday that he would be willing to meet with Pezeshkian during the U.N. gathering. Beyond either mediated or direct discussions during the event, another potential outcome of this week’s General Assembly meeting is a United Nations Security Council Resolution regarding the Iran conflict, Vaez says. Unlike a memorandum of understanding like the one the U.S. and Iran previously reached, a security resolution would be legally binding by international law and would compel both parties to abide by certain conditions in the war. Adopting a resolution would require the votes of at least nine out of the 15 members of the security council. And as a complicating factor, the U.S.—as well as the other four permanent members—would have the ability to defeat any such measure by casting a veto. “This is a very important week at UNGA for regional states, as well as the Iranians themselves, to try and roll back or manage how things evolve,” Valki tells TIME. “The President is very clearly weighing and considering the military options that are on his desk, which include not just responding to the Houthis, but another round of serious and severe strikes against the Islamic Republic.” If a direct exchange between the U.S. and Iranian delegations does unexpectedly occur during the gathering, she says, “it's a big deal. It's a big outcome. It means that the Iranian president is willing to risk his standing at home, attacks at home, in order to stave off a serious military attack and build a direct lifeline to the Trump team.” Up until now, both sides have sought “maximalist outcomes with minimal concessions,” Valki says. But Iran, through direct conversation with the U.S. delegation, could “signal a more transformational type of policy outreach, rather than the defensive reaction of traditional hardliners in the country.” On Trump’s end, Vaez says the President could potentially make concessions to Iran by removing naval blockades on Iranian ports, rolling back sanctions against Tehran, or returning access to certain frozen assets. “That's what the President can put on the table … The most important thing for Iranians is economic reprieve and that's precisely where President Trump has tremendous leverage.” A security council resolution, meanwhile, “could provide a degree of assurance about the sustainability of a new understanding between Iran and the U.S.,” Vaez says. He adds that through such a resolution, the U.N. could also establish an “observer mission”—what he describes as “an established mechanism that could look into disputes and adjudicate” if there are disagreements over alleged violations of the resolution, as there were previously over June’s memorandum of understanding. Vaez stresses, though, that the odds of this week’s gathering resulting in a meaningful breakthrough in the stalled negotiations appears unlikely. “There are very serious differences, and there's deep mistrust,” he says. “And so that's why I'm not very optimistic.”

Why Older Americans Are Drinking More Alcohol—Not Less
2026-09-21

Why Older Americans Are Drinking More Alcohol—Not Less

TIME | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings The U.S. is in the midst of a temperance boom. Drinking rates have been steadily declining nationwide, with an August Gallup poll showing that only 54% of Americans ever consume alcohol—sharply down from 67% as recently as 2010. Now, a study in the Annals of Internal Medicine takes those findings a step further, reporting that younger adults in the Millennial and Gen Z cohorts—people born from 1981 to 2012—are driving that trend. Meanwhile, older Americans, aged 50-64, are actually consuming more alcohol than they used to, with rates increasing by more than one third since 2018. “It’s encouraging to see alcohol use decline for the first time since the start of the pandemic,” said lead author Dr. Brian Lee, a hepatologist and liver transplant specialist with Keck Medicine of USC, in a statement that accompanied the release of the report. “However, use remains above pre-pandemic levels and certain groups continue to have concerning trends.” Lee and his collaborators conducted their work by sifting through the responses from more than 114,000 U.S. adults who, from 2018 to 2024, participated in the National Health Interview Survey—an annual study conducted by the U.S. Centers of Disease Control and Prevention. The survey consists of several face-to-face interviews throughout the year. In the current study, researchers looked at whether and how much respondents drink; heavy drinking, for instance, is defined as five or more drinks per day or 16 or more per week for males, and four or more drinks per day or eight or more drinks per week for females. Americans saw a 2% drop in overall drinking and 8% in heavy drinking in just the 2022 to 2024 window. Total drinking dropped most dramatically among Gen Z, with rates falling nearly 6% from 2022 to 2024, compared with about 2% for Millennials. Heavy drinking fell by roughly a fifth in both generations. Older Americans, however, went in the opposite direction, with those in the 50 to 64 age group seeing an increase of 4% in overall drinking, and 36% spike in heavy drinking from 2018 to 2024. The reasons for the changes are not clear, but greater public awareness of the health risks of drinking may play a role in alcohol’s overall decline. Alcohol has been linked to 62 different diseases, according to the Harvard T.H. Chan School of Public Health, especially liver disease, cardiovascular disease, and various forms of cancer. But not all Americans are equally aware of alcohol’s health harms. Past Research from 2024 found that far more younger people know that alcohol negatively affects health compared to older adults. Some of that knowledge may track back to the COVID-19 pandemic. “There was a lot of messaging around young adults who were dying more from alcohol-related harm during the pandemic,” says Lee. “So they could have been targeted for this.” It’s seniors, however, who could most use the same kind of public information, especially as their drinking rates go up—because the older people get, whether they drink or not, the more likely they are to contract most diseases. “This age group faces some of the greatest risks of harm from alcohol, including cirrhosis and alcohol-related cancers,” Lee said in the statement. Public information campaigns are just one of the possible causes of the drop in drinking among young people. It’s also possible that Gen Z and Millennials are simply swapping one intoxicant for another. “The idea of substitution is a leading theory,” says Lee. “With the legalization of cannabis, are people using that rather than alcohol?” And sometimes, it might simply come down to money—which most older folks have in relative abundance compared to younger people. “Younger adults in general have less disposable income [to spend on alcohol] than middle-aged or older adults,” says Lee. Reducing drinking—or cutting it out altogether—may ultimately be a self-reinforcing habit, as social expectations shift in a newly temperate demographic. “When fewer people around you drink, there is less and less societal pressure,” says Lee. “Older adults grew up in an era where everyone was drinking heavily around them and it was acceptable, whereas that's no longer the case among Gen Z or Millennials.” Whatever the causes of our society’s new sobriety, the less alcohol anyone consumes, the better. Young people may have tumbled to that truth first, but the older age groups among us  would benefit from doing the same.

What Russia’s Parliamentary Election Results Mean for Putin and the War
