The Book Claiming to Teach Couples How to Select Their Baby’s Sex
2026-09-21

The Book Claiming to Teach Couples How to Select Their Baby’s Sex

The Atlantic | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
When Christina Kattula, a 31-year-old yoga teacher in Michigan, was ready to start a family in 2024, she hoped for a son. Her husband “wanted a boy to bond with,” she told me. “And if we had girls later, he thought it would be cute to have a protective-big-brother-type thing.” Kattula decided to try something she’d heard about on a podcast: the so-called Babydust Method. The theory, which was first laid out in a self-published book from 2016, claims, contrary to scientific evidence, that couples can influence their baby’s sex by precisely timing when they have intercourse. Why not try it? she thought. What’s the harm if it doesn’t work? Kattula and her husband followed the book’s advice, and she became pregnant with a boy. During her pregnancy, Kattula posted a TikTok to her few hundred followers recommending the book to other couples. “Blessed either way,” she said in the video. “But it worked for us. If you’re on your last kid and you’re really, really trying for something, read The Babydust Method.” She’s one of many women on TikTok and elsewhere on social media who credit Babydust with helping them conceive a child of their preferred sex. Some acknowledge that the method is scientifically unfounded, while other posters are more credulous: In one video, Julie Wise, a mom of two in Massachusetts, said that it’s “not like a fact; I’m pretty sure it’s just like a theory,” but that she is nevertheless “kind of a believer.” In Wisconsin, a day-care owner and mom of four named Courtney Raspor said the method has something like a “90 percent success rate.” Some go a step beyond what the book recommends, into the realm of the woo: In Australia, Faye Briotti said that in addition to timing when she had sex, she also kept pink crystals and salt by her bed, and painted her nails pink. A woman named Reagan, who’d had two boys and was hoping for a girl, said that she got rid of all her baby boy clothes, “because I really wanted to put out into the universe that I wanted a girl.” Read: The best parenting hack is being rich The rise of TikTok and other social-media platforms has given audiences ready access to folk-science life hacks of all kinds. People can now more quickly find vertical videos about scientifically suspect practices such as mouth-taping and cortisol-lowering than they can make a doctor’s appointment to address snoring or weight gain. Conceiving and carrying a pregnancy—high-stakes experiences that are full of uncertainty—seem to make some women unusually susceptible to dubious strategies. Kathryn Taylor, the author of The Babydust Method, got the idea when she was preparing to conceive her first child. She wanted to see if she could increase her odds of having a boy—she thought her husband would be more excited about the baby if it was. She is not a medical doctor and doesn’t have a Ph.D.; she has a bachelor’s degree in microbiology, immunology, and molecular genetics, and now works in pharmaceutical sales. But she knew that an embryo’s sex is determined by the sperm that takes part in creating it: If the sperm carries a Y chromosome, the embryo will be a boy; if it has an X chromosome, the embryo will be a girl. And so she wondered if she could encourage a Y-bearing sperm to fertilize her egg. She read studies about reproduction on PubMed, a medical database, and came away believing that, after a man and woman have sex, Y sperm undergo an activation process called capacitation faster than X sperm do, but that they also deteriorate sooner. Therefore, she posited, having sex as close as possible to the day the woman ovulates should give boy sperm the best chance of getting to the egg first. By the same logic, having sex a few days before ovulation would allow the boy sperm time to die off before the egg arrives, and increase the likelihood that an X sperm will fertilize the egg. Sandra Carson, an ob-gyn and reproductive endocrinologist at Yale who has written about sex-selection efforts since the 1980s, and Michael Zinaman, a reproductive endocrinologist in New York, told me that X and Y sperm don’t move or activate at different rates, that one doesn’t live longer than the other, and that the timing or frequency of intercourse does not determine the fetus’s sex. Taylor told me she was “100 percent” sure the method was going to work for her. As she writes in her author bio, she had “a boy first and a girl second, just like she planned.” She decided to write the book, she told me, after a few moms who had “three boys or whatever” asked her how she had managed to get one child of each sex, and after she shared her tips, she said, they were successful too. She set out to create a short, readable guide—the Kindle version of Babydust is 82 pages long—to help other women. Taylor has claimed that the method works about 87 percent of the time; more than 50,000 people now belong to a Babydust Method Facebook group, which requires proof of book purchase—$10 on Amazon—to join. From the September 2026 issue: The great romance slump When I asked Taylor about Carson and Zinaman’s research, she stuck by her theory. She told me that doctors might not understand the method because they work with patients rather than researching sperm. (Carson has researched sperm extensively.) Taylor emphasized that she thinks a crucial difference between X and Y sperm is their capacitation, and even if that’s proved wrong, she said, it “doesn’t debunk the theory.” It would just mean, she believes, that male and female sperm are distinct in a way “that’s microscopic, that’s genetic, and it’s deep inside the cell.” Attempts to naturally influence a baby’s sex have a long history. Hippocrates believed that having intercourse immediately after a woman’s period would increase the odds of conceiving a son; doing so “a good while” later, he thought, would favor a girl. In the 1960s, an ob-gyn named Landrum Shettles hypothesized that Y sperm are faster but more fragile, and that to conceive a boy, a couple should have sex as close to ovulation as possible. The following decade, a public-health researcher named Elizabeth Whelan proposed entirely different advice: that couples seeking to conceive a boy should have sex four to six days before ovulation. Babydust is the updated, Millennial-pink gloss on some of these old ideas. In her book, Taylor argues that although the essence of the Shettles method is correct, the science behind it was wrong. The reality is that none of these ideas are proved to work, and neither is Babydust. At least, they don’t have a better success rate than the 50 or so percent chance a couple has of conceiving either a boy or girl anyway. Zinaman said that if techniques such as Babydust were sound, ob-gyns and fertility doctors would tell patients about them. But, he said, “we don’t have anything we can use to assist patients that way.” The only process that can guarantee having a boy or a girl is in vitro fertilization, which allows parents-to-be to choose the sex of the embryo that gets transferred into the mother. (American doctors are allowed, but not encouraged, to offer IVF for non-medically-necessary sex selection.) This is now such settled science that the most recent definitive study on the topic is from 1995, when researchers recruited 221 women who wanted to become pregnant, tracked their hormones and dates of intercourse, and found that “the timing of sexual intercourse in relation to ovulation has no influence on the sex of the baby.” The author of that study, Allen Wilcox, who once led the Reproductive Epidemiology Group at the National Institutes of Health, told me that no further studies have been conducted about these methods because “you can’t get a paper published saying there’s no association, because every scientist knows that.” Read: The problem with child-led potty training One reason some people might believe that Babydust is worth trying, despite the lack of evidence, is that it offers a sense of control. All the stages of bringing a baby into the world—trying to conceive, being pregnant, delivering the child, caring for a newborn—can inspire magical thinking in some parents, precisely because so much is out of their hands. One study of hundreds of women undergoing fertility treatments found that 40 percent of them performed a good-luck ritual on the day of their procedure; another, of pregnant Jewish women in Israel, found that they scrupulously avoided buying baby furniture before the baby’s birth in order to avoid “tempting fate.” When my son was still breech at 37 weeks of pregnancy—when most babies have already flipped into the head-down birthing position—my ob-gyn recommended a website of the most batty methods imaginable to get him to rotate. This is how I, a skeptical science writer, found myself standing on my head in a swimming pool one morning and having a “heart to heart” with my fetus about the importance of turning. (This did not work.) It doesn’t help that the first question people typically ask a mother-to-be—Are you having a boy or girl?—is something she has no power over. Many Babydust adherents with whom I spoke and whose videos I watched didn’t seem to believe in Babydust as much as they wanted to believe in something. “People are hoping to alter fate,” Zinaman said, “and half the time it alters fate and they’re happy, and the other half, Oh well, it was never 100 percent anyway.” It can’t hurt to try was something I heard often. Raspor, the Wisconsin day-care owner who used Babydust to conceive a girl, told me, “There’s no way to know whether it was the method or simply chance,” but, “I personally don’t see the harm in giving it a try, as long as you understand that there are no guarantees.” At other points in history, and in certain cultures, many parents hoped for a boy rather than a girl. Today, though, that broad preference seems to have shifted. The Economist reported last year that American and some European couples who have a girl first are more likely to stop at one child than couples who have a boy first, and that adoptive parents are willing to pay a premium for a girl. Taylor told me that, according to nonrepresentative surveys she’s conducted, two-thirds of her method’s practitioners are hoping to conceive a girl. Thus, another factor likely playing a “huge role” in this trend is sex preference, Brittany Stahnke Joy, a social-work professor at East Tennessee State University, told me. Although precise numbers aren’t available, Stahnke Joy estimates that 30 to 40 percent of expectant parents at some point experience a strong desire for a certain gender and some sadness if they find out they’re having the other. The women I spoke with seemed to sense that they’re not supposed to have sex preferences. Wise, the mom and content creator in Massachusetts, used Babydust when conceiving her daughter three years ago, after learning about it on a pregnancy-app discussion board. “Obviously, a healthy baby is the main goal,” she told me, “but we can’t deny that we do have preferences sometimes.” Kattula, the Michigan yoga teacher, expressed misgivings about the concept of trying to influence a child’s sex, even though she was pleased to be having a boy. “Is it effed-up to pick and choose what you think the gender of your kids should be?” she said. “I don’t know.” A number of desires might motivate sex preferences and gender disappointment, Stahnke Joy said: With their child, a parent might hope to recreate themselves, or a relationship with their father or mother; or they might look forward to certain activities that they can imagine doing only with either a daughter or a son. Most of the time, she said, these feelings resolve once the baby is born, but “some people have a lot more children just to try to get the girl.” This is partly why, as much as I know Babydust doesn’t work, I also understand wanting to believe. When I was pregnant, I initially wanted a girl. I thought girls were smarter and more verbal, their games were more imaginative, and they were better behaved, even though intellectually I realized this was not always true. I wanted to play Fairy Princess Unicorns instead of Garbage Trucks. Two and a half years later, I do play an awful lot of Garbage Trucks. I sometimes get jealous when I see a toddler girl sitting patiently at storytime while my son, Evan, runs wildly around the room. But Evan has also upended some of my expectations about gender: He is very talkative, has a great sense of humor, and is extremely smart. He loves stories and singing, and even getting dressed up occasionally. I sometimes wonder if, had I gotten my wish and conceived a girl, my hypothetical daughter would have any of those traits or interests. I realize that I don’t care, because I love my son exactly the way he is. Maybe the true magic dust of parenthood is that your baby will surprise you, no matter what their sex turns out to be.

Part of why I read is plot/story/character, which generally survives any edit process. The other part of why I read is creative word-choices and sentence structures. Authors like Douglas Adams made deliberately convoluted structures that amused. The style of an author is - to me - an important thing. Can LLM editors today accept and accommodate the voice of an author? No. Will they ever? Almost definitely. Where I'm at is that these tools need to be better-than-human before they should be deployed on creative material. This isn't just spell-checking, it's editing, which does involve understanding of the material and what an author is doing and why, and accepting that the results don't always fit the norms and shouldn't. Creative material is about pushing boundaries into new frontiers, and while LLM will probably get there, it's not there today and I don't want it blending my favorite authors' into some beige hellscape. The Authors Guild confirms [authorsguild.org] HarperCollins' $5,000-per-title arrangement, split equally with participating authors. Crucially, it was opt-in, and the author's payment did not count against their advance. The Guild also criticized the publisher's 50% share. PW reports approximately 15 other author-led lawsuits and roughly 10 publisher suits [publishersweekly.com]; those remain the publication's estimates, rather than an independently reproduced case count. The 63% figure comes from survey respondents reporting their employers' AI use [publishersweekly.com]. It does not measure how much publishing work AI performs. The certification paragraph needs the most care. Human Authored explicitly uses identity verification and contractual self-certification [authorsguild.org]. It does not scan a manuscript and prove that a human wrote it. Meanwhile, Created by Humans describes itself as an AI rights licensing platform [createdbyhumans.ai]. Trump Went So Far This Time That Even Fox News Won’t Stand for It
2026-09-21

Trump Went So Far This Time That Even Fox News Won’t Stand for It

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
This story is adapted from the Slatest newsletter. You can sign up here to get it in your inbox every day. Liberals have long accused journalists of letting Donald Trump walk all over them, lying to reporters’ faces and using them as political punching bags even as he basks in the glow of their camera lights. But the press’s posture may now be changing. After Trump announced he was banning three news outlets from the White House, the major TV networks—including Fox News(!)—acted as one to protest the president’s decision. The dustup carries important implications for the First Amendment and journalists’ ability to tell the rest of us what’s going on inside this administration’s inner sanctum. With a Truth Social post, as Trumpian brouhahas often do. On Friday afternoon, the president put out a lie-filled, haphazardly capitalized post announcing he’d banned CNN, MS NOW, and Politico from the White House. Trump claimed the ban was “a result of their constant ‘reporting’ FAKE NEWS” and pledged that “Other Fake News Media Outlets” would get the same treatment. By Saturday, reporters from those three organizations were being denied White House access. CNN, MS NOW, and Politico jointly sued the administration, arguing that the ban “could not be a more direct assault on the First Amendment.” On Monday, the administration barred CNN from the “pool,” a rotating crop of TV journalists who film presidential events. So the other four major networks—ABC, CBS, Fox, and NBC—decided to suspend the pool indefinitely. Media outlets unaffected by the ban, like the Atlantic and the Washington Post, issued statements of solidarity. What’s lost when reporters aren’t allowed into the White House? In fairness, most reporters still are. Print, online, and radio journalists continued to follow Trump as normal today. But even though everyone has a camera in their pocket these days, the TV pool is how news outlets around the world get footage of presidential events in sensitive areas, like Trump’s Oval Office meetings with world leaders or appearances aboard Air Force One. Without it, the only footage the public will see from those places is what the White House wants it to. The administration has teased that it could soon replace poolers with its own video feeds, which would effectively amount to state television. “The public has a vital interest in receiving accurate, independent information about its government,” the five major networks said in a joint statement. “No administration should restrict a news organization because it objects to its reporting.” A federal judge named Timothy Kelly will hear the three banned outlets’ lawsuit. Trump appointed Kelly in his first term, but the judge sided with CNN in 2018 after the White House tried to yank a reporter’s press credentials. Kelly has scheduled a Wednesday hearing, which could make Trump’s ban short-lived. The president has preemptively vowed to appeal any adverse ruling. In the meantime, the ban is already having an effect. On Monday, Trump cut the ribbon to open his new White House helipad, but the TV pool was missing and the administration’s feed was inaudible, resulting in footage of the president speaking over the sound of static. Deciding which journalists deserve access to the White House isn’t an easy question. The press briefing room has a finite number of seats, and who should get them has been a contentious question under many administrations. But this is a naked effort to punish outlets for coverage the president doesn’t like—and it’s likely aimed at having a chilling effect. Being on site helps reporters cultivate sources and break news, which in turn helps inform those of us who don’t have access to the building. If covering the president aggressively risks getting your outlet banned, it pressures reporters to pull their punches or shy away from stories that could anger the administration. Trump has already used the bully pulpit and lawsuits to try to wring more sycophantic coverage out of the media. “There’s something wrong with a country that can allow people to write purposely negative stories,” he said in the Oval Office on Friday, shamelessly trampling the founders’ vision of a country in which a free press was sacrosanct. Trump’s ban is another attack on that freedom, and it’s unlikely to be the last. Looking for an evening escape from the latest erosion of our constitutional rights? My colleagues have some suggestions. Meet the men who still love Bitcoin: Cryptocurrency’s power has declined, but it still holds a powerful allure to some people disillusioned with America’s economic system. Nathaniel Moore went to Las Vegas to see why some lost boys are still pinning their hopes on Bitcoin. Read about a beloved author’s new novel: John Green, author of classic young-adult books like The Fault in Our Stars, hasn’t published a novel in a decade. Now, he’s back with his first-ever book for adults. Laura Miller writes that Hollywood, Ending is potent “enough to make you wonder why anyone ever thought his earlier books were just for kids.” Raise a glass to a classic cocktail: The margarita’s ingredients may be simple, but its origin story is far more complicated. N.C. Stevens digs into the drink’s history and tells a fascinating tale about Prohibition and the delicious outcome of cultural fusion between the U.S. and Mexico. Watch a scary new film: Video games are notoriously difficult to adapt to other media, but, according to Sam Adams, the new Resident Evil movie makes the leap from console to screen “with the right mixture of fidelity and irreverence.” And, if you’re a gamer but not a horror connoisseur, Nadira Goffe helpfully breaks down whether Resident Evil will be too scary for you, using Slate’s highly scientific Scaredy Scale. Whether you’re watching with excitement or hiding under the covers, I hope you have a great evening! See you tomorrow. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. News and Politics - Slate Magazine
Does James Talarico Have a Chance?
2026-09-21

Does James Talarico Have a Chance?

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
Also from Jim Newell, the Surge is your weekly political sanity check: What matters, what might, and what’s just political theater. Sign up here to get it in your inbox. In 2018 a captivating congressman from El Paso nearly turned Texas blue. In a race against Sen. Ted Cruz that captivated Democrats nationwide, Beto O’Rourke lost by a modest 2.6 points, the closest a Democrat has come to winning a Senate or governor’s race in Texas in modern memory. Although this performance was still a loss that earned Democrats exactly zero Senate seats, it augured a bright future for Democrats in the state. “Blue Texas,” foretold since the Obama era, seemed on track. That perception grew in 2020, when Joe Biden lost the state to Donald Trump by only 6 points in a presidential year, improving 3 points on Hillary Clinton’s loss in 2016. The state’s color was turning. And then, in 2024, Texas Democrats, as O’Rourke told me, “got what they deserved.” Kamala Harris lost the state by a whopping 14 points. While the country shifted rightward as a whole, the turn against Democrats among Latino voters turbocharged the collapse in Texas. Cruz easily won another term in the Senate. “Blue Texas” had never seemed further away. Which makes it all the stranger that only two years later, we’re doing this again. Because Democrat James Talarico really could win a Texas Senate race. The political-media industrial complex has been down this road before. No one—no reporter, operative, commentator, analyst, or person on the street—wants to look like an idiot by overhyping it again. I certainly don’t. But after weeks of speaking with politicians and experienced campaign hands in Texas, there’s no denying it: There’s more to this race than the usual hopium. “You’d have to work pretty hard to not recognize that this is different,” University of Texas professor James Henson, director of the Texas Politics Project, told me. “That this Senate race is different than any Senate race we’ve seen for a couple of generations, let alone any statewide race.” He noted that in the run-up to the 2018 Texas Senate race, O’Rourke never led in any polls. Meanwhile, Talarico has led or tied in all but one recent poll. “I think Talarico is just one of, if not the, best candidates we’ve ever seen,” O’Rourke himself told me. Democrats have not won a statewide race in Texas since 1994. Especially given their setback in 2024, it was clear going into 2026 that Democrats would need a perfect storm to win. They just might get it. The national environment is at Democrats’ backs. Talarico is a compelling and well-funded candidate—albeit one not without baggage—running against a scandal-plagued and poorly funded opponent in Attorney General Ken Paxton. The strong gains Trump made among Latinos in South Texas over the issues of cost of living and immigration are reversing over the issues of … cost of living and immigration. There’s a populist revolt over data centers and Flock cameras. Gas is expensive. Everyone is mad. But it’s not just the conditions. There’s something more fundamentally different this time in Democrats’ approach. What struck me as I was talking to Democrats about this cycle is that, unlike in previous runs that had their hopes up, they’re not relying on vibes to do their work for them. The past failures and overhyping has chastened them and offered much to learn from, and they may well be learning it: They’re not promising a new era of Democratic Party rule in Texas. They’re just focused on winning this race, this time, and not squandering the opportunity that has fallen into their laps. Democrats’ future—not just in terms of winning control of the Senate in 2026, but in terms of staying competitive with Republicans in the long run—depends on their learning to win in red states. Have they finally cracked the code? Why does the idea of Texas turning blue hold such fascination in Democrats’ hearts? Why don’t charismatic Democratic candidates from Mississippi or Tennessee make the cover of Vanity Fair, rake in record amounts of money, and capture Democrats’ national attention? It’s not just because Texas is the crown jewel of red America, the second-largest state in the country with the second-biggest allotment of electoral votes. It’s because, unlike many other comfortably red states, there was a period when its demographics were shifting in a way that aligned with those of the Democratic Party. During the Obama years, the state was becoming younger, more concentrated around metropolitan areas, more diverse, and more white-collar. This was the coalition that Democrats were betting on for transformative change across the country, the party’s then recipe for an enduring majority. The idea, then, was not that Texas Republicans were going to convert en masse to voting for Democrats. It was that their majority would be diluted away as Democrats registered more and more Latino voters, assured to be on their side. College-educated transplants would flood to the orbits of Austin, Houston, and Dallas. Suburban voters would veer away from the GOP. The appropriate way to interpret this information was, and is, that Democrats had the ability to make Texas a competitive state. The inappropriate way to handle it was, and is, to think that assumed demographic change guaranteed that Texas would inevitably turn blue and it was only a question of when. This latter stance can breed an idleness that, as we’ve now seen, ensures it may not happen at all. One of the first tests of this era of demographic promise was the 2014 governor’s race, when Greg Abbott ran for election against Democratic state Sen. Wendy Davis. Davis had gone viral in 2013 for a 13-hour filibuster against a state legislative bill restricting abortion. She had a compelling biography, as someone raised by a single mother who rose to graduate from Harvard Law School. Helping her along in the field was Battleground Texas, an organizing group founded in 2013 by former Obama aides with the goal of registering Democratic-leaning voters in the state. While Battleground tried to explain that it was playing a long game, its linkup with Davis, who was attracting extraordinary national attention, applied pressure on both to show immediate results. But it was an awful election year for Democrats nationally, and Abbott defeated Davis by 21 points. “That was partly our fault,” Davis told me in an interview of the way expectations got out of hand. “Because when you’re running a campaign, you do need to build belief that it’s going to be different this time.” She didn’t have as forgiving a stance for the group that helped her along: “The unfortunate thing is there were a lot of big promises and hopes made by Battleground in that cycle,” she added, “that, when they didn’t come to fruition, some of the folks leading that effort just moved on.” Perhaps given that hangover, the O’Rourke campaign started off with low expectations in the 2018 cycle. “The O’Rourke campaign was not at first built to win,” Jeff Rotkoff, a Texas Democratic strategist who directed campaigns for the Texas AFL-CIO in 2018, told me. “In fact, they were dismissive of efforts of others to help them win.” Indeed, they were dismissive of Rotkoff’s own efforts to help them: He described to me how O’Rourke initially missed out on the AFL-CIO’s endorsement because his campaign hadn’t asked for it. The whole operation had a general “too cool for school” attitude early in the campaign, Rotkoff said, in which it rejected opportunities to work with the rest of the party infrastructure. But then it grew into a proper movement—not just among liberals nationally, but in Texas as well. And yet, to some degree, O’Rourke’s campaign was still winging it. Texas Democrats I’ve spoken to over the years have a way of framing O’Rourke’s 2018 Senate run as not only a highly impressive achievement … but also something they’re comfortable Monday-morning quarterbacking. There’s a pervasive sense that maybe he could have won if he’d just done a few things differently—though what those things are is up for debate. Part of the problem, everyone seems to agree, is that the party infrastructure itself wasn’t prepared for a competitive statewide race. The gubernatorial candidate, Lupe Valdez, “was sort of a sacrificial lamb” in the governor’s race against Abbott, as Henson put it. And the primary focus of the national Democratic Party was on flipping several suburban House seats, not on driving O’Rourke across the finish line. Rotkoff wanted to be clear that these weren’t attacks on O’Rourke personally, and that “they learned a lot of lessons” in real time. The point of comparison, though, is with the Talarico campaign, where “the strategic political analysis was there from the start.” This time, as former Texas Democratic Party Executive Director Manny Garcia told me, Talarico “doesn’t bear the burden of having to do it by himself.” Democrats have recruited a candidate for every state and federal race on the ballot, no matter how doomed the contest, to ensure that persuadable voters everywhere are hearing the message. (Republicans have derided this move as a gimmick.) The state party is cooperating with the Texas Majority PAC—in other words, with George Soros’ dollars—to provide resources. And Democrats have landed a strong gubernatorial recruit in state Rep. Gina Hinojosa, who sits next to Talarico in the state Capitol, to both challenge Abbott in a difficult year—making him work unusually hard for his fourth term—and reinforce the message of corruption the two candidates are using against their opponents. Talarico is “bringing so much interest to Texas, lots of money to Texas, and it helps me and my appeal to certain constituents. So we are complementing each other in a way that is very helpful for the statewide ticket,” Hinojosa told me in an interview. “There is this synergy happening up and down the ballot that we have never had before in Texas, and I do think that is a difference-maker.” So Democrats have the candidate. They have the environment. They have the opponent. They have the ticket. They have the money. They even have the strategy. One is hard-pressed to find a more dramatic shift in voter behavior between 2016 and 2024 than that of Latino voters in Texas. Hillary Clinton, per exit polls, won this cohort 61 percent to 34 percent in 2016. Eight years later—and with Donald Trump remaining precisely the same person he was, saying the same things he’s always said, every day, over the course of that stretch—Trump won 55 percent of Latinos in the state. Results on county-by-county levels are jaw-dropping. As the Washington Post reported after the 2024 election, 13 of the top 15 counties in the country with the largest rightward swings between 2016 and 2024 were in Texas. They’re all majority Hispanic. Inflation and economic hardship, which led to fond remembrances of the Trump economy from 2017 to 2019. Chaos at the border. Working-class Latinos joining the broader working-class turn away from the Democratic Party. Voters being another generation removed from their newness to the country and attaining a more conservative socioeconomic status. Social conservatives responding to leftward shifts among Democrats on cultural issues. Name something, and the answer is probably yes. This helps explain Democrats’ losses, from Davis to O’Rourke to Harris: The party may have gotten the demographic shifts it was expecting, but it never translated into the political coalition it predicted. As James Henson put it to me, “The growth of the Latino population relative to the Anglo white population was going to be a lot more complicated than the demographical-destiny trope really took into account.” And just about every Democrat and Republican I spoke to for this story said that Democrats took these voters, particularly in the Rio Grande Valley, for granted—“based on the color of their skin or their country of origin,” as Beto O’Rourke put it. A better way to put it, though, is that Democrats never had these voters secured in their coalition the way they thought they did. “Latinos felt for a long time like Democrats were the lesser of two evils,” Sergio García-Rios, an assistant professor at UT and director of polling for Univision, told me, “but that Democrats never deliver.” Democrats “thought, for a long time, that the only thing that mattered was immigration, to Latinos, and immigration reform.” But immigration reform never materialized, anyway. And then, after the parade of inflationary and border-security maladies under Biden, it was time to give something else a shot. “There’s a lot of people who are working-class in these areas who voted Democrat for a long time, and their life hasn’t gotten any better,” Chuck Rocha, a veteran Democratic strategist—who is advising both Talarico and Hinojosa this year—told me. “And then along comes Donald Trump, who sounds a lot like, if you close your eyes, Bernie Sanders,” Rocha added. “He’s talking about a rigged system. He’s talking about remanufacturing jobs in America. That’s kind of how Donald Trump controlled that space while Democrats don’t spend any money juxtaposing that position, because there’s no real party infrastructure in McAllen or in Brownsville. “And that’s the perfect storm,” he concluded, “that put us in a position where not all of those people automatically came on board like their mothers and grandmothers.” In the long run, Democrats’ ability to win back Latino working-class voters will hinge on their ability to win back working-class voters more broadly. But as far as 2026 goes, the upside for Democrats with Latino voters’ swingy voting preferences is that they can swing right back from Republicans’ 2024 highs. And they can even do so largely on the same issues with which Trump won those voters: the cost of living, and immigration. Manny Garcia, formerly of the Texas Democratic Party, told me that “people wanted something in economic terms that was being promised” by Trump. He added: “And right now, what people are seeing is, ‘Well, my prices aren’t going down like you promised, I’m not having a better economic outlook like you promised, and I am getting consistently disrespected. My family’s getting consistently disrespected. My community’s getting consistently disrespected.’ ” On immigration, Brendan Steinhauser, a Republican strategist and former campaign manager for John Cornyn, noted that Latinos in South Texas “want a secure border, don’t want the cartels to run rampant, we don’t like human trafficking, we don’t want to see immigrants dying in the desert of thirst.” At the same time, though, the current Trump administration has gone well beyond what they find acceptable. Now they also realize they “don’t want these excesses, don’t want the federal government to come in and round up everyone, especially people that don’t have a criminal past.” As Steinhauser put it: “There’s not a lot of support among Hispanic Texans in South Texas for doing mass deportation of people who are not violent criminals.” The polling shows clear signs of a reversion, with polls of Latinos in Texas backing down hard from Trump’s 2024 margins while Talarico enjoys strong support among them in his race against Paxton. A recent survey from Univision and YouGov showed Talarico leading among Latinos 56 percent to 37 percent. This flip is worth understanding specifically. Because it’s not that all of the voters Trump picked up in 2024 suddenly, in the words of Rocha, are “going to run up and become democratic socialists or start dyeing their hair blue or get a nose ring.” It just means that some of those who felt that promises made to them were broken could sit this election out. “Not all of those guys are coming back, and they still think that Democrats are queers or weak,” he added. “But they’re really pissed at Republicans.” As much as this is Texas Democrats’ best opportunity in modern memory to win a prestigious statewide race, it is, ultimately, only that: an opportunity. And James Talarico is a long way from winning this race. Talarico has been ahead in polls over the summer, with strong support from independents, but he’s rarely polling above 50 percent. The great question is whether Texas Republicans will come home for their candidate, warts and all. Their candidate has behaved confusingly. Ken Paxton has been running an anti-campaign at times so baffling that it’s worth considering whether his ability to get others to do his work for him is actually genius. To quote his felled primary opponent, Sen. John Cornyn, “Paxton’s a poor campaigner” who’s “lazy and doesn’t raise money. And he basically is not offering a very compelling case for his election.” As of the latest filing deadline, which covers the first two quarters of the year, Paxton had only $16.7 million (with joint fundraising committee hauls factored in), while Talarico had raised $72 million. Paxton was literally on vacation in London, famously the home of the British monarchy, with his girlfriend on America’s 250th anniversary. When he does bother to campaign, he makes peculiar choices, such as at a late August event for which he wore a Four Seasons Orlando–branded golf shirt and what appeared to be a $40,000 watch. All this, of course, is layered atop his history of having been impeached by the state House, having faced an untold number of corruption allegations, and having been divorced by his wife on “biblical grounds.” That he’s made it this far is because he’d remained in Trump’s favor after trying to overturn the 2020 election through a lawsuit hastily dismissed by the Supreme Court. What Paxton must have known, though, is that once he became the nominee, he’d be golden. National Republicans can’t afford to lose his race—outside money would have to swarm to prop him up. And, sure enough, the cavalry has finally arrived. After a lengthy finger-pointing match between Trump, Senate Republicans, and Texas donors about who was responsible for rescuing Paxton, they’re all chipping in. Between the recent commitments from Trump’s super PAC, Senate Republican leaders’ super PAC, and others—including Elon Musk—Paxton managed to close the spending gap with Talarico in a week. That money will allow Republicans to amplify Talarico’s most prominent weakness: his history of getting carried away during what is now called “Woke 1.0.” In 2020 and 2021, Talarico said that “God is non-binary,” that there are six biological sexes, and that “white skin gives me and every white American immunity from the virus” of racism. More recently, CNN uncovered an appearance by Talarico in a 2020 Sierra Club forum in which he said that he was “thankful that young activists have really drawn the link between climate change and White supremacy and capitalism in our country,” describing them as “oppressive systems” in need of “dismantling.” Talarico has tried to dismiss old comments like these as “cringey.” But with the right ad budget behind them, they could be just cringey enough to send Republican-inclined voters, noses held firmly, to pull the lever for Paxton. Then again, it’s quite something to see nearly $100 million in super PAC money dumped into Texas, in September of an election year, to save a Republican Senate candidate. The GOP’s late attempted rescue of Ken Paxton coincided with the Republican midterm convention in Dallas in early September, and Paxton’s role in this showcase was largely to serve as the butt of a joke. He himself gave lackadaisical remarks on the convention’s first night—alongside everything else, Paxton is also an unenthusiastic public speaker—to an indifferent crowd. For the rest of the convention, he was subjected to Trump’s regular insults about how he dresses poorly and looks bad. Paxton, waiting for his super PAC money to arrive, just stood there and took it. If Texas Democrats can’t beat this guy, in this environment, could they ever have a chance in Texas? It’s a question for Democrats in states across the country this year. Each red state in which Democrats are competitive in 2026 is a little different. Ohio and Iowa are less diverse but have more recent Democratic DNA in them, having voted for Barack Obama twice. Right now they’re being hit hard by Trump’s trade (and real) wars. Alaska is uniquely reliant on the federal government and has a large independent streak. What Texas, Alaska, Iowa, and Ohio all have in common is the national wind at their backs, strong candidates, and decisions by Trump that went out of their way to make life in their states more difficult. They also all prompt a lot of heartburn about races over the past decade that fell short despite initial signs of promise and alluring polling numbers the September before the election. Should Democrats get swept in all these races, under these dreamlike conditions, Democrats nationally may, once again, forswear ever getting emotionally or financially invested in these states. (Or at least for another two years, when they find themselves inevitably tempted again.) The issue is that soon, converting red states where Democrats have lost their foothold—not just fantasizing about converting them—will become a necessity for the party. Republicans already have a structural advantage in the Senate with their strength in small, rural states, giving Democrats limited options and narrow paths toward a Senate majority. And after the 2030 census, Democrats are going to need some fresh electoral votes. States like California and New York are projected to lose multiple congressional districts, while various other blue states will likely lose individual seats. This gives Democrats fewer Electoral College options and will officially retire the old “Blue Wall” pathway to victory of winning Michigan, Pennsylvania, and Wisconsin (not that this pathway has been the most reliable recently). That means Democrats will need more options to win a presidential race. And there’s no better place to make it up—and beyond—in one fell swoop than Texas, to where many of those blue-state congressional districts will be transferred after the census. “We damn sure better be investing in Texas now than in 2032,” Wendy Davis told me. In other words, the long-term dream of “blue Texas” from the Obama years, seen as a way to lock in an inevitable, prolonged Democratic era, could soon become the medium-term need for “blue Texas” just for Democrats to keep up. The first order of business, though? Well, you’ve just got to win a race. Get the Surge in your inbox each Saturday. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. The Theory Behind Government’s Cruelest Paperwork
2026-09-19

The Theory Behind Government’s Cruelest Paperwork

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-19

Visit Original Website
Gabfest Reads is a monthly series from the hosts of Slate’s Political Gabfest podcast. This month, David Plotz talks with journalist Annie Lowrey about her new book The Time Tax: How the Government Wastes Our Time—and How to Fix It. In this excerpt, Lowrey explains the “ordeal mechanism theory,” the decades-old idea behind why some government benefits are designed to be difficult to access, and why she argues it ends up failing the people who need help most. This partial transcript has been edited and condensed for clarity. David Plotz: I had never heard of this concept in your book and I was like, ‘That’s an amazing concept.’ Can you just describe what the “ordeal mechanism theory” is? There’s some context in which I guess it could be right. But no, this turns out to be wrong because very often people who are very vulnerable, very low income just can’t make it through the ordeal. They don’t have phones, like up-to-date phones; they don’t have computers; they don’t have Wi-Fi; they don’t have stable addresses. Plus, their lives are really stressful, so it makes it really hard to get through things. But anyway, so that’s the ordeal mechanism theory.” Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. America Hates A.I. Donald Trump Loves It. What Is Going On Here?
2026-09-19

America Hates A.I. Donald Trump Loves It. What Is Going On Here?

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-19

Visit Original Website
The Surge is your weekly political sanity check: What matters, what might, and what’s just political theater. Sign up here to get it in your inbox. Welcome to this week’s edition of the Surge, Slate’s politics newsletter that welcomes Mitch McConnell back to the Senate. Is Lindsey Graham next? If there was but one thing that happened this week, it was politics. New Hampshire, sulky and brooding, thinks it’s too “cool” to participate in a Democratic-wave election. Did Pete Hegseth almost get impeached this week? A Texas Republican candidate caused a proper foofaraw after getting deeply upset by a photo of brown people enjoying a football game. And, please, we are still welcoming feedback on the changes around this newsletter. Let us know. But first: How much are these killer robots paying Donald Trump to like them? (Actually, that’s probably discoverable in public filings …) Last week’s calls from inside the house that A.I. companies are at risk of losing control of their monsters and killing everyone—true or not!—have continued to sweep the news. Congress rolled up its sleeves and got to work devising plans for slow-moving task forces that may or may not ever materialize, before leaving D.C. until November. The salience of A.I. concerns has skyrocketed overnight. Polls show deep concerns among the electorate about A.I. advances. This isn’t going to abate as an issue over time, either. With each passing day, A.I. will become a bigger and bigger part of everyone’s lives. Soon, you won’t be able to spit without hitting an A.I. agent coming at you with a knife. (Or, well, showing you a helpful recipe using ingredients you have sitting around the house.) In short, we’ve never seen a bigger layup issue for populists, left and right, heading down the stretch of an election season. Which makes it all the stranger that the guy who ushered in the new populist era, Donald Trump, doesn’t have it in him to say a cross word about these machines at all. He’ll only chastise those who do. “I am the Hoax Buster, and I’m right now breaking another Hoax—That AI is going to take over, consume, and destroy the World, and that Robots will be marching into our Cities, and getting rid of us all!” he posted this week. “This is even wilder than the RUSSIA, RUSSIA, RUSSIA HOAX, or the Global Warming Scam.” In a separate screed regarding the need to regulate A.I., Trump posted that the “only control or ‘guardrails’ that AI needs is a STRONG AND SMART (High IQ!) PRESIDENT, and the U.S.A. has that, in spades!” He called the A.I. warnings a “SICK conspiracy” and warned: “Conspiracy Theorists, Treasonists, Traitors, and Leakers, BEWARE!” All of this follows a late-August post responding to the data center backlash when Trump chastised voters for their concerns. “If we kill the Golden Goose,” he wrote then, “you will only have yourselves to blame.” Why can’t Trump just ride the wave here, when he’s usually so good at co-opting shrieking public panics? A few thoughts come to mind. You could say he’s genuinely concerned about being outmaneuvered by China on A.I. and that this is informed by classified briefings he gets on the matter. But he just doodles pictures of hamburgers during those. More important to him is that the tech people are nice to him, always bringing him gifts and purchasing protection by donating to his political action committees. What’s also important to him is that A.I. buildout has been responsible for most, if not all, of the GDP growth under his administration. The “Golden Goose,” then, is the Trump economy’s paper gain. It’s that last part that’s so critical given Trump’s psychology: He views the attack on the engine of growth he presides over as an attack on him. Comparing the A.I. backlash to the “RUSSIA, RUSSIA, RUSSIA HOAX” is as close to a tell as there is. There haven’t been many bad polls for Democrats across the board since Labor Day, as pollsters shift to surveys of likely voters instead of registered voters and capture the Democratic-enthusiasm edge. (Required note of caution: Sometimes polls are wrong as hell!) But there’s one race that’s standing out, much as the likeness of an old man’s face once did on Cannon Mountain. Some recent surveys show Democrat Chris Pappas and Republican John Sununu, who served in the Senate previously, locked in a tight contest in New Hampshire’s Senate race. And this week, the Cook Political Report shifted its race rating to “toss-up.” What’s going on here? Sure, New Hampshire’s not the bluest state in history, but it should be comfortably blue enough for a Democrat to win in this environment. And Pappas, a sitting congressman, should be a perfectly acceptable head of hair to complete the task. The most obvious issue is Pappas’ opponent’s last name. The Sununus are one of New Hampshire’s royal families, and John’s brother Chris is a popular former governor. Republicans have also outspent Democrats in the race as they attempt something of a sneak attack, with plenty more coming in ad reservations this fall. Republican Gov. Kelly Ayotte, meanwhile, is cruising to reelection, and could carry Sununu along with her. We thiiiink this will sort itself out based on fundamentals, and that Republicans are mostly trying to get Democrats to spend money to defend it? But there are always anomalies in even would-be wave years, and Voters might think the Senate candidate is his brother is the stuff from which anomalies are made. It’s not a situation that Democrats can ignore anymore, at least. California Gov. Gavin Newsom told CNN’s Jake Tapper while fly-fishing (?) that he would not run for president if Kamala Harris ran. He wasn’t even backed into this corner. This was just two of the fellas out there fishing, and Newsom said it with barely any prompt. “Why would I?” Newsom said, after Tapper responded with a huh? “I wouldn’t do that to her, I wouldn’t do that to you. I wouldn’t waste everyone’s time.” What that tells us is that he must believe, or know, that Harris won’t run. Because there’s no way that Newsom is not running for president. No way! He also told Tapper that he would have to check to make sure a run would be OK with his family, but they all say that—the family is always reportedly enthused (unseen, off-camera). Another possibility is that Newsom will do a sneaky li’l thing like quick-announcing his candidacy before she has a chance to announce hers. Because he’s clearly thought about how to beat her. When Tapper said that the argument against Harris would be that she’s had her shot and lost, Newsom didn’t miss a beat. “Yeah,” Newsom said, “and, I mean, that would be the approach to the campaign, certainly. That would be the case we’d make. A pretty good one, actually. That’s objectively true.” Watch out for these sneaky Californians, folks. For an extra section of lightning-round political thoughts from Jim, sign up to get the Surge in your inbox. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. www.theregister.com - Articles
Alibaba Cloud plans six-year stroll to 20GW of datacenters, reveals chip to power them
2026-09-22

Alibaba Cloud plans six-year stroll to 20GW of datacenters, reveals chip to power them

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-22

Visit Original Website
USA alone has 37GW under construction, but Alibaba doesn't think the AI boom will run out of steam Chinese tech giant Alibaba has outlined an ambition to expand its datacenter fleet to 20GW of capacity, and chip that will help it get there. CEO Eddie Wu announced those ambitions today at Alibaba’s Apsara conference, where he delivered a speech that likened current AI applications to light bulbs, because electric light was an early application of electricity but the really important stuff came along decades later. “Steam and combustion engines were designed merely to do what horses and laborers were already doing: pumping water, weaving, and hauling,” he said. Over time, the CEO said, engines proliferated to the point at which “machine power already drives 99.9% of the world’s physical work.” The CEO thinks AI – which he prefers to call “Machine Thinking” – will one day do 99.9% of all cognition, and usher in various utopian outcomes. “In the future, every niche domain will have millions of AI scientists and domain experts constantly driving breakthroughs and tackling challenges,” he said, before asking his audience to imagine an AI charged with building a starship capable of reaching Mars. “For such an ultra-complex, long-horizon task, AI will break it down into tens of millions of subtasks, executed by millions of agents working non-stop until completion,” he said. “A human only needs to define the intent and the goal to mobilize massive intellectual resources.” Alibaba Cloud , he said, has decided to play its part by mobilizing resources to scale its global datacenter fleet to 20GW. By way of comparison, commercial real estate outfit Cushman and Wakefield last week said it can see 37.7GW of datacenters currently under construction in the USA alone. Some of that will go to the Stargate project, which has promised to bring 10GW online by 2029. OpenAI is a member of Stargate and has promised to implement 10GW of Broadcom accelerators by 2029. Meta has announced plans to build a 5GW datacenter campus for its own use. In late 2025, Amazon said it added 3.8GW of capacity in the previous twelve months. We could go on but you probably get the idea: Alibaba Cloud is going to build a lot of infrastructure, but perhaps more slowly than its rivals – and may still end up with a relatively modest datacenter fleet. The company will, however, apparently fill its bit barns with China’s most advanced AI chip – the Zhenwu V900 Wu announced the processor in his keynote and described it as “the most powerful AI chip in China today, delivering three times the performance of its predecessor, the Zhenwu M890.” Apparently, Alibaba can build a single cluster packing up to half a million V900s, and the resulting machine will “power frontier model training and inference.” The chip comes from Alibaba’s T-Head semiconductor business, which has published basic specs describing the processor as possessing 216GB of memory and 1200 GB/s inter-chip interconnect bandwidth. Machine translation of the spec sheet explains the chip possesses the following qualities: Native support for FP32 through FP4, and optimized for cutting-edge AI models and AI application workloads; An improved Tensor Core arithmetic unit that significantly improves instruction precision in FP8/FP4 formats; Richer scaling factor formats and Block Size configurations under MXFP8 and MXFP4, which apparently make for more stable performance when running training and inferencing workloads. Wu said he expects “significant growth in the annual AI chip shipment volumes,” but didn’t say when the chip will go into production, or when Alibaba will make enough to power one of its giant clusters. The CEO also used his speech to announce that Alibaba has started training its next-generation model, Qwen 4, and has two successors on its roadmap that it expects will scale to five and ten trillion parameters respectively. Alibaba may develop those models using Recursive Self-Improvement (RSI) – the technology that sees models design new models. “Currently, Alibaba’s Qwen team is exploring RSI and has made meaningful progress,” Wu said, before mentioning that the team plans to train models with the same number of parameters as the planned Qwen successors. One matter Wu didn’t touch on is where Alibaba plans to build its new datacenters. The company is China’s top cloud and has a colossal domestic market to address. Alibaba Cloud has also targeted growth in Southeast Asia and has presences in Europe and North America. New datacenter builds face considerable community opposition in all those locations, and Chinese tech firms are often viewed with even more suspicion than their US counterparts. China, meanwhile, is promoting construction of giant datacenters in the country’s west where renewable energy is plentiful and cheap. Gartner predicts 55 percent of enterprise VMware users will be investigating an exit by 2029
2026-09-22

Gartner predicts 55 percent of enterprise VMware users will be investigating an exit by 2029

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-22

Visit Original Website
Chasing pack has its problems too, with maturity, cost, and complex licenses Analyst firm Gartner believes over half of VMware users will start evaluating alternative hybrid cloud platforms yet still rates the Broadcom business unit a leader in two major markets. Gartner predicted increased interest in VMware exits in its Magic Quadrant for Distributed Hybrid Infrastructure (DHI) – aka hybrid clouds – which opens with a “Strategic Planning Assumption” that “By 2029, 55 percent of enterprises will initiate proofs of concept for alternative distributed hybrid infrastructure products to replace their VMware-based deployments and embrace hybrid cloud infrastructure delivery, up from 25 percent in 2026.” Gartner’s mention of 2029 feels noteworthy, because that year will be six years after Broadcom’s acquisition of VMware. As The Register has previously written, plenty of VMware users acquired fresh three-year subscriptions in the months before the deal closed. Migrating from VMware to a rival platform is a risky and complex project, and we often hear of customers deciding it is safer to stick with Broadcom for longer while they hatch plans. If orgs in that position refresh for another three-year sub during 2026, 2029 would be a natural jumping off point. Numerous virtualization contenders are trying to lure Virtzilla’s customers, yet progress is slow. Nutanix CEO Rajiv Ramaswami has often said it will be several years before some are ready to move. While Gartner predicts a majority of VMware customers will contemplate a move, the Broadcom business unit will keep most of its customers for at least another three years – and recently turned its attention to refreshing its low-end vSphere Standard product, a move that will give it a chance to retain more current users. The DHI Magic Quadrant rates VMware as a leader in the field, alongside AWS, Nutanix, Microsoft and Oracle. Gartner rates VMware’s core virtualization technology, formal sovereign cloud ecosystem, and AI-native infrastructure as strengths. The first VMware weakness Gartner mentions makes for sobering reading: “VMware has experienced an increased level of negative sentiment from Gartner customers, particularly regarding communication, commercial business practices and delays in support, compared to other market leaders.” The firm had similar things to say in another Magic Quadrant – this one dedicated to Server Virtualization Platforms. On this MQ, Gartner rated VMware as a leader in the field and praised its technology and AI integration. But the firm also felt it necessary to report negative customer sentiments. “Gartner clients have reported that the transition to per-core subscriptions has resulted in significant cost increases. Many have also reported minimal flexibility during negotiations, which has forced many heads of I&O to actively evaluate migration alternatives.” VMware isn’t alone in copping some criticism. On the DHI Magic Quadrant, Gartner states “Nutanix’s licensing and pricing models can be complex and less competitive than other market leaders” and notes that AWS’s on-prem Local Zones “carry a 15% to 35% price premium over their parent regions in expensive metros” and come with “substantial cost premiums.” On the server virtualization quadrant, Gartner warns that Nutanix “licensing can be complex and pricing frequently exceeds expectations. Clients should carefully evaluate the ROI of potential migrations.” The firm warns would-be Microsoft users that “Administrators must navigate multiple disjointed management consoles, including Azure Portal, Windows Admin Center and System Center Virtual Machine Manager, to perform routine operational tasks.” Gartner hasn’t published a server virtualization Magic Quadrant for a decade, so this year’s document includes some new players. HPE scored “Challenger” status, as Gartner feels its products lack important features and the company is yet to establish a track record in the field. Proxmox, which The Register often hears mentioned as a VMware alternative for low-end users, scored Niche Player status due to lack of support for top-tier enterprise applications and its low headcount making the availability of support uncertain – a factor the outfit recently addressed by adding a North American office and 24x7 support. ® USA alone has 37GW under construction, but Alibaba doesn't think the AI boom will run out of steam Chasing pack has its problems too, with maturity, cost, and complex licenses PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity Public pushback puts bit barn resource use under the microscope War damage, air traffic chaos, and a storage shortage expose the cost of treating resilience as someone else's problem Of 225 flaws found by Glasswing and tracked by VulnCheck researcher, just one has confirmed exploitation in the wild Fueled by the AI boom, the House of Zen's rise isn't just Instinct - Lisa Su is riding high on some Epyc design chops too 'Humans are responsible, not the AI,' argues Scott Bessent as he calls out OpenAI agents' hack of Hugging Face 'A new category,' insists the Chocolate Factory, not a premium Chromebook - yet some of the family resemblance remains Forrester predicts operators will face tariffs, grid commitments, and tougher community scrutiny Seats, Flex Credits, and all-you-can-eat contracts make for an 'anxiety-filled architecture' PLUS: US takes down Iranian propaganda sites; Marketing company asks 'Why Do We Have Your Information?' California tightens datacenter rules on water and power
2026-09-21

California tightens datacenter rules on water and power

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
Public pushback puts bit barn resource use under the microscope California Governor Gavin Newsom on Monday signed seven state bills that require datacenter operators to report more info about their operations, and to involve communities as they plan future facilities. The California Assembly Bills (AB) and Senate Bills (SB) signed by Newsom require greater disclosure of datacenter water and electricity use, address who pays for grid connections and other power infrastructure, and introduce rules covering electricity rates, water resources, and environmental review. They include: California state senator Steve Padilla, who authored two of the bills, said the bills will ensure that datacenters pay for energy grid connection, for electricity generation costs, and for a larger share of wildfire mitigation and liability costs. The governor's office cited the Trump administration's dismissal of Americans' concerns about AI and datacenters to emphasize Newsom's willingness to constrain an industry of major significance to California's economy. "While the Trump administration moves toward deregulation, communities are left to deal with the consequences — higher electricity demand, grid constraints, water use, and pollution," said Newsom in a statement. "Today we are once again laying the groundwork for a stronger approach, because we know that we don’t have to sell out Californians or sacrifice our well-being to innovate and succeed. California has proven that time and time again." Dr. Nathan Wangusi, who runs the independent water data transparency platform Data Center Water Leaks, sent a statement to The Register in which he observed that California's new disclosure requirements could improve transparency around how much water datacenters use, help communities understand the bit barns' impact on local water supplies, and protect ratepayers from infrastructure costs. Much has been said about the need to regulate the safety of AI services, and the environmental impact of the datacenters that host them. The White House has pushed to accelerate datacenter deployment with executive orders and President Trump has insisted regulation is not necessary. Yet datacenters have proven unpopular with local communities, who often express concern about water consumption, the potential for increased noise, pollution, and energy prices, plus general unease about AI's effect on labor and society. According to the National Conference of State Legislatures, 16 states are currently considering whether to ban datacenter construction. An even larger number of statehouses are exploring some form of datacenter regulation. In its Q2 2026 report, Data Center Watch said, "Thirty statehouses introduced, and many adopted, legislation, resolutions, or executive actions addressing datacenter siting, electricity and water constraints, and infrastructure cost-sharing." The watchdog group estimates that at least 45 projects worth roughly $68 billion were blocked or delayed in the second quarter of 2026. Last year, Newsom vetoed a datacenter water bill, AB 93, reportedly in response to tech industry lobbying. In a statement [PDF] explaining his decision last October, he said, "...I am reluctant to impose rigid reporting requirements about operational details on this sector without understanding the full impact on businesses and the consumers of their technology." Newsom's willingness to sign an almost identical bill suggests that popular discontent with datacenters has escaped the gravity of lobbying and that AI will be a flashpoint in the upcoming US midterm elections. The seven bills Newsom signed into law are: AB 1577 by Assemblymember Rebecca Bauer-Kahan (D-Orinda). Data centers: reporting. AB 2383 by Assemblymember Rick Chavez Zbur (D-Hollywood). Electricity: data centers. AB 2469 by Assemblymember Diane Papan (D-San Mateo). Data centers: water use disclosures. AB 2619 by Assemblymember Diane Papan (D-San Mateo). Water resources: datacenter. SB 886 by Senator Steve Padilla (D-San Diego) and Senator Jerry McNerney (D-Pleasanton). California Technology Innovation and Ratepayer Protection Act. SB 887 by Senator Steve Padilla (D-San Diego). California Environmental Quality Act: environmental leadership development projects: data centers: geothermal power plant projects. SB 1168 by Senator Jerry McNerney (D-Pleasanton). Anthropic-linked CVEs pile up, attackers mostly shrug
2026-09-21

Anthropic-linked CVEs pile up, attackers mostly shrug

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
Of 225 flaws found by Glasswing and tracked by VulnCheck researcher, just one has confirmed exploitation in the wild Despite the concern that advanced AI models’ bug-hunting prowess will lead to attackers exploiting more newly uncovered CVEs, fewer than 0.5 percent of the vulnerabilities linked to Anthropic or Project Glasswing are being batttered in the wild, according to VulnCheck security researcher Patrick Garrity. Garrity began tracking CVEs attributed to Project Glasswing, Anthropic’s initiative to give select partners access to its Claude Mythos Preview model, shortly after the AI company announced the program in April. At the time, Anthropic said the new model was too risky to release publicly because its bug-finding and exploitation skills surpass all but the most skilled humans. As such, Anthropic restricted access to Mythos Preview to vetted Glasswing participants, who use the model for defensive security work, including finding and fixing flaws in their own software products and open source dependencies. Garrity’s Anthropic CVE tracker maintains a list of vulnerabilities credited to the Anthropic team and/or Project Glasswing and also checks these CVEs against the company's known exploited vulnerabilities index "to get a better read on the real Glasswing ‘danger factor.’" As of Monday, the CVE count is 225, and just one, a critical SQL injection bug in Ghost (CVE-2026-26980), has been exploited in the wild. “There's a big difference between finding vulnerabilities and whether they're actually useful to and will be used by threat actors,” Garrity told The Register. “The main thing this data highlights is that what Anthropic is discovering and disclosing is fairly limited in impact, and from what we can tell, isn't resulting in different outcomes from a threat perspective than a random selection of other vulnerabilities would.” Anthropic didn’t immediately respond to our questions, but we will update this story if we hear back. Garrity says he doesn’t dispute AI’s ability to find bugs. Indeed, anyone following security disclosures over the past few months would have a hard time arguing that AI models aren’t bringing to light significantly more security flaws than ever before. Case in point: recent massive patch drops from Microsoft, Apple, Palo Alto Networks, and don’t even get us started on open source projects. Also, as Garrity pointed out, these vulnerability-finding skills aren’t “a capability unique to one model or harness.” “A lot of the hysteria we're seeing assumes that every vulnerability or bug is likely to be used by threat actors,” he told The Register. “But the reality is that only a small fraction ever get used in exploitation campaigns. Historically, that's ranged from just under one percent to two percent of vulnerabilities that get weaponized and used in the wild.” Plus, while recent AI models excel at finding bugs, they still aren’t great at fixing them, as a couple of recent studies have highlighted. In one of these, 1Password’s research team produced and analyzed 6,080 patches developed by two frontier models: OpenAI's ChatGPT-5.5 and Anthropic's Opus 4.8. The models generated fixes that fully resolved the vulnerability just 26 percent of the time, while about 54 percent either failed to resolve the vulnerability, introduced a new vulnerability, or did both. Another study by app security shop Veracode found that across more than 100 models and 80 coding tasks, the average security pass rate for AI-generated code was just 56 percent. This all means that the work involved in developing and applying security fixes still requires humans. “The bar for vulnerability discovery is much lower with AI, but the real gap lies downstream in coordination, triage, remediation, and patch deployment, which is still largely people-intensive work, as Anthropic itself has acknowledged,” Garrity said. “It appears they might not have realized this until after they launched the project.”® USA alone has 37GW under construction, but Alibaba doesn't think the AI boom will run out of steam Chasing pack has its problems too, with maturity, cost, and complex licenses PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity Public pushback puts bit barn resource use under the microscope War damage, air traffic chaos, and a storage shortage expose the cost of treating resilience as someone else's problem Of 225 flaws found by Glasswing and tracked by VulnCheck researcher, just one has confirmed exploitation in the wild Fueled by the AI boom, the House of Zen's rise isn't just Instinct - Lisa Su is riding high on some Epyc design chops too 'Humans are responsible, not the AI,' argues Scott Bessent as he calls out OpenAI agents' hack of Hugging Face 'A new category,' insists the Chocolate Factory, not a premium Chromebook - yet some of the family resemblance remains Forrester predicts operators will face tariffs, grid commitments, and tougher community scrutiny Seats, Flex Credits, and all-you-can-eat contracts make for an 'anxiety-filled architecture' PLUS: US takes down Iranian propaganda sites; Marketing company asks 'Why Do We Have Your Information?'
